# A.19. Complementarity and coherence

The preceding entity is the Instituto Costarricense de Ferrocarriles (ICFC), created by the law No. 7001, dated September 19th of 1895 in Costa Rica. Costa Rica's state-owned railway company was established as a statutory corporation to operate the country’s railway network.

# A.20. Executing Entity information

The Ministry of Finance (MCF) is the loan recipient and thus the Government of Costa Rica is the executing entity.

# A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages given)

In 2015 Costa Rica had total GHG emissions of 9.4 MTCO₂e, of which the transport sector contributed 51%. Emissions from road transportation have increased by 38% since 1990 due to population growth and urbanization measures. The public transport share has declined in the last decade from 64% to 41%; while car use and the hailing services have increased significantly. The main reasons for this are: lack of investment in public transportation, high congestion and agglomeration. Alone congestion costs are estimated at 4% of GDP in the 2018 Non-Motored Transport Study (NMT). The Ministry of Public Works (MPW) has proposed a Light Rail Transit (LRT) system for San José being cited as main reasons. Barriers towards a new transport system are primarily of financial nature plus limited experience and capacity to implement such projects. In order to improve last-mile connectivity and the modal split, Costa Rica is in a critical situation of public finances and needs to find solutions that can change its transportation system.

The LRT project will be environmentally sustainable, safe, and environmentally sound electric Light Rail Transit (LRT) along the central East-West axis of the large urban zone of San José with 65 km of double tracks on 5 lines transporting annually 83 million passengers. The LRT will serve a population of approximately 40% or 52% of Costa Rica’s population. But people could spend up to 60% of their time in traffic. The LRT will use the existing rights-of-way, low cost, and low maintenance infrastructure. The project will replace 18 diesel trains with 30 electric trains, which will reduce CO₂ emissions by 95%. The LRT will also replace 24 track diesel train and replace it with an environmentally friendly, high capacity LRT. This design incorporates best practices in urban planning and transit-oriented development (TOD). It is a project that seeks to improve the integration with the existing bus-based public transport system. NMT and improved LRT connectivity and accessibility will be achieved by providing 100% of the population living within 500 meters from an LRT station. The integration with the existing bus-based public transport system, NMT and improved LRT connectivity and accessibility will be achieved by providing 100% of the population living within 500 meters from an LRT station. The project seeks to improve the integration with the existing bus-based public transport system. NMT and improved LRT connectivity and accessibility will be achieved by providing 100% of the population living within 500 meters from an LRT station.

The project is in urban development project transforming urban mobility into an ultra-low emission system. The LRT is the backbone of a new public transport system that seeks to improve last-mile connectivity, and accessibility for transportation. Fostering of public transport and electrifying the transport sector are the main strategies to achieve the goals of the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) 2015-2030. The LRT project will contribute significantly especially “good health and well-being”, “sustainable cities and communities” and “climate action” whilst also proving for new jobs during construction and operations.

The project is estimated to cost US$4,897 million (LC$ 1,852 billion). NMT/Connectivity interventions 20 MUSD and capacity building, gender and monitoring 3.1 MUSD with an ERR of 22% result of time savings, lower emissions, reduced accidents, and improved quality of life. The project will be financed by a loan from the IDB for 40%, of its total cost with fare-revenue wherewith the remaining part is based on public funding, which is common for urban transport projects in Costa Rica. The concessionaire will finance the remaining 60% of the project cost and shall be through a Public Private Partnership (PPP) under a 35-year concession (design, build, operate, maintain and transfer contract). # GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 5 OF 9

A proposal for a $500 million fund to be funded by annual payments for services. The GCF contribution and its concessionally full-floated loan to the government and reduce the financial burden only of the Government of Costa Rica and not of the private sector. This project is a public-private partnership (PPP) that includes both public investments in NMT and connectivity components as well as capacity building, monitoring and gender specific action measures. The total leverage ratio is 5:19 and the private sector leverage ratio is 4:3 if the project successfully pulls-in private investment.

The project will reduce GHG emissions by 20 million tons of CO2 equivalent (CO2e) over a 30-year lifespan with a GCF cost per ton of CO2e) reduced from $36 to $18. Costa Rica produces more than 98% of electricity by renewable energy sources and the project will further reduce emissions through the use of electric vehicles, which significantly reduces urban air quality, reduces acceleration sustainability, increases trip time, reduces gender inequality and has positive social and economic benefits.

The LRT is a key component to urban transport emissions and is a core element of Costa Rica's national decarbonization plan with the goal of net zero emissions in 2050. It is also a component of the Post-COVID19 Green recovery Plan, which aims at creating short-term jobs and long-term social and economic benefits achieving a transformational change to a greener economy.

# PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate context (max: 100 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Context: Climate Change

In 2015 Costa Rica had emissions of 10.9 GtCO₂e, of which the transport sector contributed 51%. Emissions of the transport sector are projected to decrease under the BAU scenario by 4%, whilst under a mitigation scenario they are projected to drop by 65% by 2050, compared to 2015 (MINAE, 2017). Fostering of public transport and electrifying the transport sector is one of the key strategies in Costa Rica’s National Determined Contribution. The country has a long tradition on fighting climate change. In its Nationally Determined Contribution, Costa Rica reaffirmed its aspiration of becoming a Carbon Neutral economy and aims for a 100% renewable energy mix by 2050 (MINAE, 2017). Costa Rica is the only country in Central America that has received from UNEP the 2019 Champions of the Earth award for its commitment to ambitious policies to combat climate change.

The Costa Rican electricity system is one of the few decarbonized systems in the world. Since 2015, Costa Rica has also had >98% of renewable energy production¹. Planned energy expansions will diversify renewable energy sources and increase the share of hydropower generation from 73% to 84%, while reducing fossil fuel generation². Specification of the transport sector is a strategy to reduce drastically the carbon footprint of the country.

### Context: Urban Transport in San José

Costa Rica has one of the highest vehicle shares per 1,000 inhabitants in Latin America (PEN-CONARE, 2018). The larger urban zone of San José called GAM (“Gran Área Metropolitana”) has 3% of the national territory and 52% of the country’s population – an estimated 2.7 million people live in this area (MINEL, 2011). The public transport system is dominated by a large number of privately-operated bus operators. A bus trip has on average a 70% longer duration than a car trip and costs about 50% more per passenger-km. In the GAM, the share of private vehicles in the city has increased significantly and vehicle operating speeds have dropped to 14–15 km/h. Congestion for public transport users is a major problem in San José. The average speed of buses is only 8 km/h due to route structuring which requires for over 40% of bus users at least one transfer (PEN-CONARE, 2018, p.23).

The National University of Costa Rica engaged in air quality monitoring since more than 2 years, estimates that vehicle emissions are the main source of PM and NO₂ pollution in San José. The average PM concentration is above guideline value of the WHO (PEN-CONARE, 2018, p. 243). PM₁₀ and NO₂ are also the air pollutants which cause respiratory problems for the population. Emissions from the transport sector of the GAM are estimated at 1.9 million CO₂e for 2014 (equivalent to 35% of total transport emissions of Costa Rica) and are projected to increase by 60%, under a BAU scenario by 2030 (Grutter Consulting, 2016).

¹ https://www.environment.gov.cr/and-storespress-resources/costa-rica-named-on-climaton-earth-pioneering-role-fighting-climate

² World Bank report of ICE: Costa Rica | Data (worldbank.org)

# Baseline

The baseline scenario is a continuation of the current bus-based public transport, a marginal share of rail passengers (<<1% of trips), and basically private means of transit. The observed trend is towards usage of private means of transportation, which are more convenient but also lead to increased traffic congestion, longer travel time spent on trips and a reduced quality of life for city dwellers. The bus-based transport network lacks coherence for urban mobility in the sense that it does not provide an integrated system with sufficient capacity and frequency being implemented. Private bus operators are locked in a competitive position, cannot agree on common approaches and are unable to invest in infrastructure improvements. As a result, public transport is losing market share while car use continues to increase strongly in the GM (Cutter Consulting 2016) as well as at the national level (MINEC, 2019).

## Mass Public Transport in the GMP

Costa Rica has made numerous investments over the last 3 decades on new mass public transport systems (see for a review of these investments). The most sustainable urban transport plan financed by the IDB and the GEF came to the conclusion that an integrated transport system with a LRT combined with a bus-based system based on an organized route network is more efficient than a stand-alone LRT or a stand-alone bus-based system. A new LRT line, the train, which is completely outdated whilst still having the right of way and the space (which is not sufficient for a BRT or a bus-based system) is not a good investment in terms of cost-effectiveness compared to other than the average of other comparable LRTs (see table 1) and an investment at par with a high-quality BRT (based on TDP GMR) while being more convenient, faster, more sustainable and having better follow-up investments.

## Related Projects and Proposals

Related transport projects and initiatives are:

- Modernization of the bus system focusing on route re-structuring, improved access, transfer stations, integrated electronic ticketing and the promotion of inter-modality. The IDB supported project forms together with the GEF project.
- National plan for electrification of transport (MINEC, 2019) with financial incentives for the import of electric vehicles.
- Various municipalities are developing project ideas for Transit-Oriented Development (TOD) and for NMT support. The IDB supported project forms together with the GEF project.
- Project to be implemented under the UNDP's Global Environment Facility (GEF) Small Grants Programme for Climate Change (SGP-GCC) in the area of Energy Efficiency and Delivery of Global Environmental Benefits starting in 2013 and administered by the UNDP is complementary to this project. The GEF project will support the implementation of energy efficiency infrastructure investments for zero emission mobility. The synergy generated between the two projects will enable the development of a more comprehensive and integrated approach to sustainable urban transport, where legal and institutional barriers will be removed with the GEF project. GCF interventions proposed in this project are also strongly complementary to existing initiatives in TOD and NMT and complement them with investments in energy efficiency.
- Costa Rica is at the moment involved in the following projects with the GCF: FP097 (Multiple Countries: Prioritization of Sustainable Urban Transport Projects in Latin America and the Caribbean - IDB Plus Results-Based Payments for 2014 and 2015), FP151 (Multiple Countries: Global Subsidized Climate Fund - GSCF - Equity). The project is also involved with the GCF's new initiative, the Sustainable Urban Transport Climate Fund (SUTrCfD - Equity). The present project received PFP support from the GCF.

## B.2 Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

### Climate Problem

Costa Rica wants to achieve net-zero emissions by 2050. The transport sector is the largest source of GHG emissions in the country. Implementing a low-carbon sustainable urban transport system is a condition for achieving the climate target. However, the current public transport system based on diesel buses with high emission (ii) lack of a convenient public transit offer in accordance with climate targets, resulting in higher carbon intensity from means of transportation and increasing emissions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.1 | PAGE 8 OF 59

## Paradigm Shifts

The major barrier towards achieving a modern, multi-modal low-carbon public transport system is that this requires a comprehensive and systemic change. The traditional public transport system is locked into offering low-cost, quick trips to the most frequent users of the system. This has led to an over-reliance on private vehicles as a result of this negative spiral. The experience of other countries in Latin America, e.g., Colombia, which had comparable per capita GDP and population sizes to Costa Rica, shows that it is possible to make a significant shift from private transport towards public transport.

The current system is not sustainable because it is based on the assumption that the demand for mobility will continue to grow out of this lock-in. A paradigm shift is required towards offering the passenger a more convenient system which is better integrated with other modes and offers a wider range of travel options, including intercity travel. This requires a significant and visible shift towards a new public transport system. Without this mode shift from private means of transport to public transport will occur. Once this mode shift is targeted by the project due to its high impact on GHG emissions, connectivity and mobility are targeted for their high mitigation impact and the long-term behavioral change potential.

The transportation sector is transformed to a sustainable low-carbon system with a dominance of public transport. The new public transport system is based on a core network of the Green Transport Plan of Costa Rica established due to the COVID-19 crisis as a vehicle for transformation towards a sustainable mobility system which creates economic, environmental and social benefits, short-term jobs and a long-term greening of the economy. This will contribute significantly to the achievement of decarbonization and net-zero emissions by 2050 and contributes substantially to SDG targets.

## Goal Statement

If this sustainable transport system is implemented then Costa Rica can be well on its way to hit its target of net zero emissions in 2050 because mobility emissions of the country are reduced significantly.

The sustainable transport system reduces GHG emissions and contributes towards sustainable development through reducing congestion, improving air quality, increasing safety and accessibility. The project will reduce traffic congestion and long trip time, as pollution and accident reduction. Direct impacts are a CO₂ emission reductions of 7.6 million tons (a reduction of 295 kt/yr), 12,000 tCO₂e/yr and 58 GtCO₂.

## Project Outcomes

The project outcomes are: (i) increased usage of a sustainable low-emission urban transport system; (ii) an increased capacity of stakeholders to replicate successfully NMT and connectivity interventions; (iii) reduced economic costs of traffic congestion and pollution; and (iv) reduced dependency on fossil fuel imports.

## Project Results

Project results are:
(i) mode shift towards LRT;
(ii) mode shift towards cycling;
(iii) mode shift towards low-carbon public transport;
(iv) increased use of NMT;
(v) improved safety, accessibility and attractiveness of stations resulting in reduced GHG transit emissions of residents living in the urban area; (vi) LRT also results in reduced road transportation accidents.

## Project Activities

Project activities are:
(i) identify and engage the concessionaires;
(ii) design, build and operate the LRT;
(iii) design, build and operate the NMT;
(iv) develop a marketing strategy for promotion purposes;
(vi) monitor project outputs, results, outcomes and impacts; (vii) provide capacity building, training and outreach;
(viii) implement the gender action plan. The MITT interventions result in 10km of new cycle lanes, 25 km of new pedestrian paths, 30 km of new bus lanes, 40 km of new LRT tracks, and a population of 140,000 persons. The investment in the LRT results in 3 new rail lines, 85 km of tracks and 46 stations integrated with other modes.

# Core Project Barriers

1. Lack of funds for public transport and NMT investments: Costs like RRT is a critical situation of public finance. The lack of funding for public transport infrastructure, as well as investments in non-transport connectivity are long-term investments with a positive economic rate of return without being financially profitable. Private investments for a Public-Private Partnership (PPP) model are not attractive to the private sector; inefficient, risk-burdened public monies are available which ensure private investors of the financial viability of the project.

2. Funding barriers: 'Financing' refers to the capital to deliver the infrastructure "funding is in the long-term necessary for the development and operation of transport systems. The funding required by urban transport systems, can only partially cover its funding needs from farebox and ancillary revenues and requires transfers from other sources. Public transport systems are often underfunded because they do not generate sufficient revenue to finance infrastructure and do not pay external costs related to accidents, congestion, and pollution. The viability of LRT is also affected by the lack of adequate funding for its operation and maintenance. This results in a reliance on public subsidies to operate transport modes including buses creating a political barrier against public transport investments.

3. Limited experience, information and knowledge on successful NMT and last-mile connectivity interventions: Less than 10% of urban areas have implemented effective strategies for NMT and PT. The train takes 8 years to construct before being operational. It is not deemed as probable that a sustained impact on preference or use for PT will influence the train or NMT.

4. Ineffective efforts to achieve gender equality: A risk which is tackled with the GAPP.

5. Preference for private modes of transport i.e. residents do not change to new modes of transport such as the LRT and NMT. The bus system is not convenient and results in very high trip times. People thus basically use the bus

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 10 OF 59

## Assumptions

1. It is assumed that finance is available for the investment part of the project and that funding is available to pay the true costs of the system.
2. The GCF plays a critical role in this assumption.

### Important Assumption
3. An important assumption is that the mode shift primarily from cars and traditional buses to the LRT and NMT occurs. The new system offers competing advantages such as a reduced trip time of 30-60% (resulting in <1 hour of travel per day) compared with the current situation, lower operating costs, and lower vehicle acquisition cost.

### Another Assumption
4. Another assumption is that the LRT has lower emissions than the baseline transport system. This can clearly be shown to be the case due to its virtually 0-carbon grid factor of Costa Rica and the much higher energy efficiency offered by the electric motors used in the LRT.

5. The project will only materialize if there is sufficient political willingness to finance and fund the project. Again, the political willingness is linked to the financial conditions which are influenced decisively by the GCF contribution.
6. CB is effective and needs to successfully technology transfer, a good design and implementation of investment strategies.

### Project Outcomes
7. Monitoring systems are implemented well and are sufficient to identify service issues and facilitate the identification of solutions to remedy these.

8. Materials and construction methods used prove to be effective against climate impacts including high temperatures, thermal oscillations and hail damage and ensure protection against flooding.

# Project Brief

The project is a modern, fast, convenient, safe, and environmentally sound LRT along the central East-West axis of the country, connecting the 4 cantons and 4 provinces of Costa Rica. The LRT will transport annually on average 63 million passengers benefiting around 2.7 million inhabitants living in the capture area of the line, representing 52% of the total population of the country.

The LRT consists of four lines: Line 1 (San José to Alajuela), Line 2 (Alajuela to Heredia), Line 3 (Heredia to Ciudad de Dios), and Line 4 (Ciudad de Dios to El Coyol). The LRT replaces the old low capacity single line that runs from San José to Heredia. The LRT is a double track line except for the first section of Line 1, which has only one track. The LRT consists of 82 double-track lines and stations and the following lines:

* **Line 1 (San José to Alajuela):** With 16 stations and a length of 37 km.
* **Line 2 (Alajuela to Heredia):** With 15 stations and a length of 29.8 km.
* **Line 3 (Heredia to Ciudad de Dios):** With 13 stations and a length of 24.6 km.
* **Line 4 (Ciudad de Dios to El Coyol):** With 7 km, 5 stations, and 2 stations are extensions of lines 2 and 3.

## Figure 2: LRT of the GAM

An old train operates partially on the proposed LRT route and the right-of-way exists on the entire tracks. The LRT is however an investment in a completely new LRT. It only uses the right-of way of the existing old track. New rail tracks are laid (double track instead of the prevailing single track), new bridges and fly overs are built (instead of at-level crossings), and new stations are constructed. The LRT will be fully electrified while the current train runs on 1 line with outdated rails and old diesel engines. The system capacity is increased from a maximum of 2 trains per hour to 4 trains per hour, which results in a train with 20x carrying capacity, a higher speed, lower emissions and more convenience – in short a new rail system that will serve the country for decades.

The LRT has an average daily ridership of around 150,000 passengers. The LRT realizes per track only between 5 and 12 services daily, with a 30-minute frequency between 5 and 9AM and 2 and 6PM. The LRT operates from Monday to Saturday during the day and on Sunday in the morning.

minute and peak at a 15-minute interval between 5AM and 11PM on a continuous basis. The current rail is clearly not a mass rapid transit (MRT) system. GIZ defines MRT as “it is designed to move large numbers of passengers in a safe, reliable, comfortable and fast manner over long distances at high frequencies.” The basic requirement of MRT is a developing city that it can carry large amounts of passengers, rapidly” (p.1) and LRT and BRT systems typically operate at average speeds of between 20 and 30 km/h. (p.1) DTFP defined rapid transit modes as: Rapid transit is a form of public transport which provides high capacity, frequency, speed, comfort, reliability, and quality of the service.” The LRT fully complies with this definition when it operates in urban areas. However, when operating outside these areas, the LRT system is not a conventional service (conventional railway) and thus is not in line with a mass rapid transit system.

The project seeks to apply the principles of improved last-mile connectivity as well as Transit Oriented Development (TOD). The TOD concept aims at creating compact urban development around its stations with its goal doubling the current population density in urban areas and by increasing it by 20-30% within a radius of 500 meters. This is achieved through the implementation of various land use strategies, such as mixed-use developments, high-density residential buildings, and commercial centers. Costa Rica has focused on the institutional and regulatory framework in order to improve the conditions of women, with the aim of favoring accelerated returns at social and economic levels. In the last issue of the Global Gender Gap Report (World Economic Forum, 2019), Costa Rica was ranked as one of the countries that most favors equality between men and women in terms of public transport users are women. The public transportation system must guarantee safe spaces and participation of women in decision-making processes. Women’s mobility is a key issue for improving their quality of life. In order to avoid costs and economic losses, the project has designed activities that promote the training of the personnel who work at the stations and the implementation of specific services for women users. These are addressed specifically in case of an attack. The project will also implement communication tools and campaigns directed towards women users. The project will also include a mechanism to ensure the safety of women, such as a rapid response system for cases of violence against women, and implement adequate infrastructure elements that allow women to access public transport services with greater comfort and security. These include the necessary spaces to ensure women’s comfort and take into account their mobility patterns (accompanied, with shopping carts, or multiple bags or packages, or strollers for children). To improve women participation the LRT will be designed in such a way that it is easy to access and use, and that it provides opportunities for directly connecting women to employment or entrepreneurial opportunities, whether they serve as providers to the institution or as users of its services. The project will also include specific activities aimed at promoting dialogue in order to implement measures and programs that take into account their perspectives and views on the design and implementation of public transport systems. Workshops and training sessions have been designed for this purpose.

The LRT shall strengthen the resilience of the city’s transport system to impacts of climate change, especially heat waves, heavy rainfall events, droughts, and changes in temperature and precipitation. The selection criteria parameters on various project components were considered at the structural, operational and functional level. processes for assessing the generation of service and functional analysis is the ability of the system to provide service consistently. In order to achieve a disaggregated risk analysis, the risk analysis was carried out by sections of the LRT project (Grutter Consulting, 2020a) and (Grutter Consulting, 2020b) that found that many of the risks in the LRT project are low and in low-medium risk categories with a few subset of medium to high risk. The risks associated with the effects of the future climate change on the LRT project are not anticipated or modeled. The risks posed by these climate change interactions can generally be considered within the design and normal operating conditions of a project. The risks to major infrastructure components include: (a) flooding, including surface water and groundwater; (b) landslides and other infrastructures; (c) damages, longitudinal and transverse; (d) bridges; (e) slope instabilities and landslides; and (f) subsidence. The risk assessment was carried out by the LRT project team in accordance with the guidelines provided in Volume 13 - Track Superstructures (all part of DOMC, 2020 Technical Feasibility Report). The construction and operation of bridges is a complex process that involves many different factors including the type and usage of materials resistant to maximum temperature, thermal contraction and hail damage, protection against flooding of the bridge structure, and the design of the bridge structure. The LRT project team has considered the effects of the rain phases and its subsequent safe evacuation. As examples sizing of bridge structures will be based on a design rainfall intensity of 100-year (50-year) period for surface water runoff and 200-year (100-year) period for groundwater runoff, respectively. The LRT project team has considered the effects of the rain phases and its subsequent safe evacuation. As examples sizing of bridge structures will be based on a design rainfall intensity of 100-year (50-year) period for surface water runoff and 200-year (100-year) period for groundwater runoff, respectively.

The Climate Risk and Vulnerability Assessment showed that the LRT design is climate-proof and incorporates all best practices for a climate resilient infrastructure. The proposed NMT and connectivity interconnections also include a number of measures to reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts.

The implementation planning foresees the issuance of the tender for the concession contract and detailed engineering studies in 2018, with construction commencing simultaneously resulting in a total construction time of 4 years. All lines shall be operational by end 2025.

Implementation shall be through a Public Private Partnership (PPP) with a special purpose vehicle (SPV) to be created for the project. The concession period is 30 years and the government share in the SPV will be approximately 45% lower than table below), due inter alia to the existing right of way. The project will be able to recover through tolling revenues, which are expected to be around USD 200 million per year. Remaining parts are covered by governmental subsidies. Most urban rail system worldwide are unable to rely on tolling revenues and on farebox revenues, let alone capital expenditures (see e.g., (EY), (SYSTRA), (2016) or (World Bank, 2018)).

**PPP Structure**

The PPP is implemented through a fully established and fully owned SPV concessionaire. The government does not have any equity stake in the SPV but uses a tolling mechanism and pays upon completion construction a fixed sum of $50 MUSD and with operations annual availability payments linked to performance criteria. This system

## Factors that influence the choice of transaction models include project implementing experience of the private partner for this type of project, Value for Money and the possibility to mobilize private financing to complement constrained public resources (World Bank, 2016).

The FPV is a form of 30 years fixed construction plus operation i.e. if the construction period takes longer than prevised then the concessionary will have a shorter operational period to recover its investment. After this time the concessionary can continue operating and recovering costs from the revenues generated by the project.

The SPVI income base is based on a fixed, regular availability payment realized by the government covering all expenses (including maintenance) of the project. The availability payment is subject to deductions in case of service failures or condition failures (condition criteria) and physical infrastructure availability (effective asset assessement). Eventually, income derived from other sources such as user fees, tolls, etc., can be considered complementary income. This means that the government assumes most of the demand and fare risk, but the concessionary is still required to be able to tender process. To achieve interest of private investors in such projects the government needs to either provide a financial guarantee or an insurance.

For the development of the project, two transaction structures or business models were analysed:

- **Model PFD - Payment by Availability and Transfer of Income:** In this model the concessionary's revenues are an income from the availability payment made by the State. The concessionary is responsible for maintenance, and profitability. This value is covered by the toll system revenues and the payment made by the State (the user fee). The concessionary can also be subject to deductions in case of service failures or user tariff. In case of a surplus in the fare revenues from the system (user fees) these are for the State. The concessionary's revenues are subject to possible deductions only in the case of service failures or lack of availability, and the tolls are not guaranteed by the National Government. The concessionary's revenue is guaranteed by the National Government for the payment of the fixed amount established in the concession contract.

- **PPDQ Model - Pay with Availability Demand Transfer:** This model transfers part of the demand risk to the concessionaries. The concessionary's revenues are determined by a fixed payment from the State and the system's performance (availability). The availability payment is subject to deductions or discounts according to the MGR (Minimum Guaranteed Revenue) established in the concession contract. The payments made by the State as in the PDD model will be subject to deductions or discounts according to the MGR (Minimum Guaranteed Revenue) established in the concession contract.

The advantages of this model is that the State's payments are stable since the demand risk is shared, which means a lower risk for the State and a higher risk for the concessionary. The concessionary can also benefit from the projections of the baseline scenario). So by assuming a higher risk the concessionaries has a higher rate of return.

The following table summarizes the main characteristics of the two transaction models.

### PPPD Model
The private sector government takes over the operation and maintenance of the project. The government guarantees a minimum income to the concessionaire, which is a very attractive alternative for financiers and concessionaires, as it is easy to bank in place and on the market, taking into account that the demand is not a risk attributed to the private party but 100% to the State and the resources to remunerate the investment. The government also has the option of adjusting the project to the requirements of the private investor.

### Business Model
The concessionaire receives three sources of income over the operation and maintenance period:
- Revenue per passenger carried: pay-as-you-go, there is a transfer of demand risk since fewer passengers carry the risk of not being able to pay for their trip. The concessionaire, however, a minimum value of demand revenue is guaranteed, i.e., the GMI demand.
- OPEX Tariff Revenue: Revenue from the provision of the transport service, which is an available payment calculated on the basis of annual OPEX costs. This revenue is affected by the optimal performance of the service.
- CAPEX Tariff revenues: revenues associated with the availability of the project covering investment and interest.

Under the PPPD model, the concessionaire receives two sources of revenue throughout the operation and maintenance period (i) OPEX Fare Revenue and (ii) CAPEX Fare Revenue; the demand risk under this model is passed on to the private investor.

Alternative transport options

Other mass transit options such as a Bus Rapid Transit (BRT) or exclusive bus lanes have been extensively studied for two decades [1], without any implementation success basically due to the complex legal and operational structure of these systems.

The public transport option is based on the LRT linked with a re-structuring of the bus-system (Grupo de Análisis sobre el Transporte Público en la Ciudad de México, 2013). The study concluded that the best option for Mexico City came to the conclusion that an integrated transport system with an LRT combined with a bus-based system based on reorganized routes and a new fleet of buses in the inner city should be explored.

In other cities such as Guangzhou already have a similar system. This has been rolled over a large part of the system. The right of way and the space are available. This space is not sufficient for a BRT or bus based system. While the current situation is not ideal, it would be possible to establish an LRT in this area. The main advantage here is that it drastically reduces investment costs (a) land purchases are limited; (b) earth movement is reduced; (c) existing infrastructure can be used and (d) there will be no need for a new fleet of buses in the inner city. It would not be possible to establish on the existing track a BRT due to lack of space.

Cota Costa therefore made various analysis (e.g., IDB, 2015) to see how the existing system could be changed to allow an LRT to be established. The results were that the LRT would have lower costs and higher benefits than the average of other comparable systems (see table 3). The average CAPEX of a gold standard BRT in developing countries is US$1,852 MUSD per km. The average CAPEX for an LRT system is US$907 MUSD per km.

The LRT investment costs of Cota Costa, BRT investments have however a shorter lifespan: buses 10 to years and roads 20 years; the LRT has a longer rolling stock life: 35 years and average infrastructure excl. rolling stock is of 50 years (IDB, 2006). Using the same criteria as Cota Costa, the CAPEX for an LRT system would be US$1,852 MUSD per km.

The advantages of an LRT are: higher capacity and frequency; better service reliability; greater passenger convenience; lower acceleration; lower emissions and more attractive for customers. The decision for a LRT is thus based on the fact that it has a lower CAPEX than BRT and other comparable systems, while meeting all the criteria.

For operational implementation the alternatives of a PPA (Public Private Financing) were assessed, performing a value for money (VFM) analysis (IDB, 2006). The VFM examined quantitative (project-level monetary) and qualitative (potential monetary and non-monetary) impacts. It also considered other risks where quantified based on the risk matrix: high risk (high probability - low impact); medium risk (medium probability - medium impact); low risk (low probability - risk event). A risk review in a total of 12 risks identified would not affect the project through PPF. The core advantage for Cota Costa in realizing the project as a PPP is more rigorous public sector scrutiny and greater transparency, which can be allocated for other vital investments, reduced risks and improved public sector efficiency.

Project Components

The project has the following components:

* 1. LRT system with a CAPEX of 1,852 MUSD including (IDOM, 2020):
    * Infrastructure and systems worth $34 MUSD with the main elements being rail, rail stations, electrification, signaling, train depot and workshops, communication systems and rehabilitation of affected services.
    * Rolling stock worth US$719 MUSD for a total of 58 trains (13 MUSD per unit).
    * Financing changes during implementation estimated at 298 MUSD.

## B.4 Implementation arrangements (max 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### Overview

CABEI is the Accredited Entity (AE) of this project and is a lead agency in accordance with the Accreditation Memorandum (dated 08/09/2017), the GCF and the AE will be responsible for the implementation of the Project. The executing entity are the Costa Rican Institute of Railways (INCOFER) and the Government of Costa Rica acting through the Ministry of Finance (MOF) for the purposes of channeling GCF proceeds. Costa Rica's state-owned railway company, INCOFER is responsible for the design, construction, operation and maintenance of the country’s railway network. INCOFER has administrative autonomy, legal personality and asset ownership.

The project implementation will be the responsibility of the Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) established by the concessionary of the train with supervision by INCOFER. The concession contract is regulated by the Law No. 7782 of Costa Rica, which establishes the framework for the concession of public services to a private entity (the Grantor) to a Private Developer (the Concessionaire) of a contract which includes the right to design, build, operate, maintain and manage the infrastructure and services related to the project. The SPV will be established under the DBOT (DBOMT) contract. INCOFER identifies and engages the concessionaires and supervises the execution of the contract through the SPV. The SPV is responsible for the implementation of the project, including the design, construction, LRT (runs, train technology, capacity, construction principles) as well as NMT and integrated connectivity stations. The SPV will be responsible for the operation and maintenance of the infrastructure until the end of the acceptance certificate and operation permit. Thereafter it supervises and controls implementation and defines if all concession conditions are met for the annual availability payments.

INCOFER has a preliminary list of interested concessionaires, which exceed 10 companies. Likewise, IDOM coordinated with these companies that the amount to be tendered and the size of the works is feasible.

## CABEI

CABEI is a supranational development bank focused on Central America, founded in 1960. The Bank's objective is to promote the economic integration and the balanced economic and social development of the Central American region. Costa Rica is one of the founder members of CABEI. To date (2018) CABEI approved projects with total costs US$3 billion, disbursed US$2.5 billion, and has a portfolio of 49 active projects. The Bank's capital stock is US$7.6 billion, authorized capital of $7 billion USD, 11.6 billion of assets and a Return on Equity of 8.9% as of end 2019*. CABEI relies heavily on the World Bank Group (WBG) for technical assistance in project preparation and implementation practices, aligned with CABEI's Environmental and Social Policy. In the transport sector, CABEI has realized financial support to the Costa Rican government through a loan agreement, which includes technical assistance through support for rail projects, among others. Within this framework, it is developing a regional plan for sustainable urban mobility in Central America.

CABEI has realized various PPPs in Costa Rica including Route 27, Vía Centrales de Alajuela, Alta de Mogotón and with ICE. CABEI will also have two supervising entities for the loan, one contracted by the government of Costa Rica and another by the World Bank Group (WBG) to ensure that the project is implemented in a timely manner and in order to have sufficient external technical know how during the entire process of PPP structuring and implementation.

## MINISTRY OF FINANCE

The Ministry of Finance (MOP) as the loan recipient on behalf of the Government of Costa Rica an executing entity. INCOFER

### The entry stage of the CABEI loan (Art. 2, 2017)

Within INCOFER there is an entity to support and assist in the implementation of the concession agreement. This entity will be responsible for the entry stage of the concession, compliance with conditions precedent, design stage and the construction stage. INCOFER will also be responsible for the preparation of the project management plan and the execution of the project management plans and in general the compliance with the obligations of the concessionary contract in the concession agreement. The entity will be an autonomous institution within INCOFER and will be a separate legal entity.

INCOFER is an autonomous institution of the Costa Rican State, in charge of managing the national railway system. It was created on September 9th, 1986, through Law No. 7001, which had several reforms, the most important being Law No. 9366 of 2015 which gives INCOFER the mandate to build an electric train in the GIM. It also allows INCOFER to operate the railway network and to manage its assets. INCOFER has been operating since it has been in charge of the administration of the railway network in the country since 1985.

Concession contracts are entered into with private parties, which have a term that can extend from 20 years up to 60 years. The concession contract for the GIM is for 40 years and the concession period extends over approximately 60 km, and mobilizes more than 3.2 million passengers annually. However, it does not represent the end of the concession process as there are other concessions that INCOFER has entered into with private parties in operation or under negotiation.

The unit also has a subsidiary company called TECO (Transporte de Costa Rica S.A.), which is an operating unit with members extensive experience in the management of concession contracts. This unit and its subsidiaries have been involved in the preparation, implementation and execution of several international bidding process to determine the private party entering the PPP will be organized by INCOFER following the regulations of the General Law on the Concession of Public Works with Public Services**.

## Implementation Structure

It is estimated that the Government of Costa Rica will have to destine a net sum of around 110 MUSD per annum for the concession period (25 years) in order to cover the costs associated with the operation and maintenance (O&M) (GSDM, 2020, pp. 8-9). This represents 0.6% of the total budget government in the year 2020 (3.8% of current expenditures).

The concession contract will be signed between the Ministry of Public Works and Transportation, INCOFER, the Ministry of Finance, the successor bidder and the concessionaire company (SPV). The MoP is to the case with all sovereign loans, is responsible for handling the national accounts and to transfer the funds to the private concessionaire. The Ministry of Finance will be in charge of ensuring that there are sufficient resources available for payment.

The Government of Costa Rica is the beneficiary of the grant for component 2 and the concessional loan of the GCF. The grant for component 3 goes to INCOFER. The private concessionaire receives a one-off payment of 500 MUSD (in 2 equal installments) upon LRT construction and lines ready for operation. This is not a loan nor an investment in the SPV but a payment for the concession period. The Government of Costa Rica will not have any concessionally do not influence in any manner the profitability of the private concessionaire as it is fixed payment. The concessional loan from the GCF will be used to finance the construction and operation costs that flow to the government and reduce the financial burden only of the Government of Costa Rica and not of the private company.

The following figure shows the project structure. The same structure is used for NMT investments as for LRT ones i.e. these investments are included within the tender document and the PPP.

The SPV will be responsible for implementing components 1 and 2 based on the concession contract. As such it does not manage, supervise or audit the implementation of the project. The SPV is only responsible for the financial management of the fund of the government for the concession contract. The loan recipient is the Ministry of Finance (MOF). MOF is therefore also an executing entity and will be responsible for making payments to the SPV at INCOFER's instructions.

The CABEI loan for this project is as of early 2021 under discussion in the Congress of Costa Rica. Only after approval can the tender process for the PPP be started. According to the schedule, the bidding process for the PPP is scheduled to start by mid-2021 and will last until late 2021. The MOF will prepare a technical report and evaluation of the bidder's proposals, an appeal process, if needed, and a stage of PPP contract negotiations. There is therefore no need for INCOFER approval as INCOFER has three legal attributions.

### Implementation Arrangements

* CABEI issues a sovereign loan to the Government of Costa Rica.
* The loan recipient is the Ministry of Finance (MOF).
* CABEI prepares the Terms of Reference (ToR) of the international bidding process for the concessionaire and directly contracts with the concessionaire.
* CABEI also prepares the ToRs of the tender process for the SPV, which will be contracted by CABEI with an international bidding process for a company with experience in the transport sector to validate the technical feasibility of the project. The SPV is responsible for the financial management of the fund directly provided by CABEI.
* Disbursements and MOF payments to the concessionaire are made after provisional entry of operations of each line (Art 6.9a).
* SPV funds shall be used in the financial statement.

## B.S Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Climate Vulnerability
Costa Rica has the 8th highest economic risk exposure to three or more climate change-related hazards. 6.5% of its GDP resides in areas at high risk of climate hazards (GFDR, 2011). The proposed project is a climate resilient urban transport system that reduces the vulnerability to climate change impacts while mitigating GHG emissions in the transport sector.

### Fiscal Situation
Two pressing development challenges stand out in Costa Rica: the fiscal situation and persistent inequality. These issues are among the basic pillars of the Costa Rican development model: inclusion, growth, and sustainability.
Successful implementation of the fiscal reform and the fiscal rule that came into effect with the 2020 budget key to preserving macroeconomic stability. The central government debt reached 1939.5% of GDP, up from 52% in 2018. The public debt ratio is projected to reach 476% by 2025 (IMF, January 2021). Costa Rica has been hit hard by the COVID-19 pandemic, notwithstanding the government's proactive response and the country's relatively low infection rate. The pandemic has led to a significant economic contraction of -8.3%, with GDP projected to contract in 2020 (IMF, January 2021). The important and immediate medical, social, and economic needs present an urgent challenge for the government.
In 2020, the country has reached a technical agreement with the IMF for a program of reforms and policies to reduce the fiscal deficit and to promote reforms which allow for lasting and inclusive growth. This serves as

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 27 OF 59

## Project Finance

The project is a PPP. The total investment of $1.862 billion USD for the LRT (including finance costs of $200 MUSD) will be financed by a combination of: (i) a 350 MUSD loan from the government (from CABEI), and (ii) a 1,302 MUSD from the private sector (of which 250 MUSD as equity). The GCF receives a one-off payment against constructed LRT lines ready for operation of $800 MUSD. This is not a loan or equity, but a payment against having committed to finance the project and providing technical assistance in its implementation.

The SP/PPJ/concessionaire does not receive a loan directly or indirectly from the GCF. The 550 MUSD by the government will be used as equity contribution for the concessionaire. The remaining $1,302 MUSD of private finance is the Government of Costa Rica which has lower financial costs and can fund the project better.

The GCF does not have an equity stake in the SP/PPJ/concessionaire but rather a loan to the government based on a profitability risk (FIRR) required by the potential concessionaires. For the PPPD, where the concessionaire takes more risk (part of the debt is transferred), a 13.5% FIRR was assumed to be expected by the concessionaire. The GCF has no equity stake in this project and does not have any financial exposure to it.

Both structures the government contribution of $50 MUSD to which the GCF contributes is fixed. The impact of GCF contributions on the cost of capital for the SP/PPJ/concessionaire is significant, as the concessionaire's cost of debt is 12% and its equity cost is 30%. However, in the PPPD structure it has a contribution to the financing part which is very important especially in light of the fact that the concessionaire takes more risk.

The projected impact of the 550 MUSD loan of CABEI on the central government debt level is by 2026 an increase of 0.65%. Whilst this is not a huge increase concessional loans from the GCF are critical for the country to implement the project and the concessionaire to finance its equity contribution.

The cost of capital for the SP/PPJ/concessionaire is significantly lower than that of the PPPD structure as the GCF is more concessional than the CABEI rate of 6-month Libor + 2.9%. CABEI does not charge commitment fee and has a loan from the GCF thereby reducing immediate cash-flow pressure on the government.

## GCF Finance Instruments

GCF finance is separated in two a loan of 250 MUSD and a grant of 21.3 MUSD. The loan is used by the Government of Costa Rica to make payments to the concessionaire. The GCF grant is 1.3 MUSD for capacity building, monitoring and gender considerations. The GCF will not be NMT or NMMI compliant.

Last-mile connectivity and NMT investments such as cycling lanes do not generate a direct income. Improved pedestrian infrastructure can reduce congestion in urban areas by increasing non-rivalry in consumption and non-excludability*. Increasing levels of cycling means improving accessibility, livability and overall attractiveness of the city for citizens plus improved air quality and reduced congestion. These benefits are difficult to quantify but are important for the long-term sustainability of the project.

The associated with investments in NMT and last-mile connectivity which results in limited government investment in infrastructure and public transport. The GCF is not designed to finance these types of significant and transformational impacts of cycle lanes are clearly in the medium and long-term with more people getting accustomed to using this mode of transport by increasing the number of LRT passengers through improved

## B

### Accessibility and effectiveness of the LRT, and by creating a behavioral change. To quantify an ex-ante the economic benefits of such interventions is complex.

Cycling projects are more successful if they are undertaken in combination with high quality public transport systems, as this creates a complementary effect, but in combination it is about a bigger change (Civitas, 2015). The LRT is thus a big opportunity to promote NMT and last-mile connectivity measures. The LRT is both a catalyst for the development of NMT and last mile connectivity infrastructure and an enabler of such measures. These elements are critical to take the project beyond a standard LRT and result in an innovative and transformative urban development model. However, it should be noted that the GCF does not have a specific mandate or funding stream for social impact, as they do not form part of the core investment program of the LRT, and the government needs to focus its limited available resources on areas with a wide and short-term impact such as the tram infrastructure.

The most important component is to ensure the impact of GCF investment and a crucial aspect for the desired paradigm shift.

### GCF Added Value

The GCF investment should reduce the fiscal burden of the government. GCF grant components create added value on (i) modal integration of the LRT linking the mass transit system with aspects of last-mile connectivity, NMT and multi-modal integration; (ii) increased social and environmental impact through last-mile connectivity and NMT measures; (iii) reduced public expenditure on road infrastructure as a result of the LRT. The GCF investment also creates potential for rail transport – other investments in mass transit systems in the region do not include aspects of rail transport, which is an important component to ensure the desired paradigm shift.

Concessional finance from the GCF is basically critical and a game-changer:

a) Funding availability: The IMF states the Costa Rica is facing severely constrained availability of external financing (tourism receipts which represent 19% of exports have collapsed due to COVID-19 in 2020) and lighter global financial conditions. This has led to a significant reduction in concessional finance from multilateral development banks. In fact, Costa Rica is facing lower than expected external loan disbursement and limited borrowing room from a small, saturated portfolio of existing loans. The GCF can provide the necessary funding for this project.

b) The long tenure of 40 years of GCF finance distributions pays over a longer period and gives Costa Rica an important breathing space and more flexibility for other investments and financing demands.

c) The GCF concessional interest rate reduces the financial burden for servicing external debt. First, it forecasted that the cost of borrowing from multilateral development banks would increase by 10% in 2020 (one of the highest in Latin America). The effective interest rate of Costa Rica in 2020 is 4.4%. (IMF Country report no 20/145 Costa Rica – April 2020).

d) The GCF 10 year grace period cannot be obtained from private finance and is very important in the current light fiscal situation of Costa Rica, which urgently required breathing space to fix the fiscal situation and get on a growth path whilst implementing fiscal reform.

### B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

[IMF 05-2020.pdf](https://ebratilings.com)

**Page 31**
---

The concession contract is for 35 years (estimated at 5 years construction and 30 years operations*) after which the facilities will be turned over to RICOFER or a new contract is established with the same or another private entity. The concession contract includes all costs of operation and maintenance, including subsidies. At the end of the concession period, the project after ending the concession period. During the concession contract the subsidizes of the Government are only for 10 years. After that, the LRT will be a fully commercialized system. Even if the concession has terminated various parts of the LRT still have a remaining life-span e.g. structural parts of the tracks and stations are expected to last in a typical system around 50 years, the signaling system is expected to last for 20 years (DOM, 2020, p. 72).

If the public transport system will require in 35 years subsidies or not is an open question. However, a low carbon mass transit system as backbone of public transport combined with NMT or emission-last-mile connectivity vehicles and other sustainable mobility options can be a win-win for all stakeholders. It provides benefits to people with a low overall environmental impact, low economic costs and a high quality of life. The LRT has a highly positive externality effect on the environment and society. This is why it is considered as a public good and should be desired* due to the positive externalities or public goods created by the project (less air pollution, less noise, less congestion, etc.). It is also expected that the LRT will be profitable for the private sector. Already today from an economic perspective the LRT is not subsidized but profitable for the country and society.

The NMT and last-mile connectivity investments are expected to result in a transformational change towards shorter trips realized increasingly by walking, cycling including electric bicycles and micro-mobility forms such as electric scooters and e-bikes. This will lead to a reduction of private car use and thus to lower traffic congestion and investments i.e. not roll them back but expand them.

*The concession period remains at 35 years even if construction takes more time

More than 100 cities worldwide have even come to the conclusion that free public transport is economically more profitable than charging a transport tariff as additional ridership and costs are as well as the subsidy of the full financial costs are outstanding. Luxembourg was the first country in Europe, and now also the first country in the world to offer countrywide since March 1st 2020 free public transport countrywide on trains, trams or buses (https://www.luxemburger Wort.lu/2020/03/04/luxembourg-is-first-country-in-the-world-to-offer-free-public-transport/)

## C3.3 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities?

Yes ☑️ No ☒

## C3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?

Yes ☑️ No ☒

### Capacity Building

NAT and last-mile connectivity monitoring of activities allows to gain insight and experience on the impacts of different measures in the area of last-mile connectivity and NAT and how they interact with ridership and passenger satisfaction. The data collected through these activities can be used for a variety of purposes, including:

- Improved data collection generates information on how to improve the quality of service.
- How to establish inclusive policies and practices that are sensitive to the needs of all users.

The monitoring and knowledge on successful interventions and their impacts are an important element to facilitate future funding decisions. The GCF is committed to ensuring that lessons learned from these activities are made accessible to stakeholders through knowledge products such as guidelines and impact reports on NAT and last-mile connectivity, workshops, and outreach activities such as webinars and workshops.

### Technology Transfer

Costa Rica has the moment no-modern, electric LRT. It has experience with rail systems but these are based on outdated diesel locomotives. The GCF investment in the LRT enables a technology transfer of environmentally sound modern electric trains to Costa Rica.

# C. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## D. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1/2 page)

The project reduces GHG emissions through the LRT itself: the NMT and last-mile connectivity activities.

### 1. LRT

Emissions are reduced due to mode-switch. The LRT is powered by electricity produced >98% by renewables. The following graph compares the well-to-wheel (WTW) emissions per km of different modes of transport.

Figure B: GHG Emissions WTW per km of Transport Modes in GAMI, 2019 (gCO₂e/km)

| Mode | Emissions |
| --- | --- |
| Car | 250 |
| Bus | 200 |
| Train | 150 |
| LRT | 75 |

Note: WTW = well-to-wheel, pkm = passenger-kilometer  
Source: (Grutter Consulting, 2020b); all emission factors based on local data

The methodological approach to determine LRT emissions reductions is based on an approved Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) project design document (PDD) and a detailed life cycle assessment methodology. (see details in Annex 22, table B including, but not limited to:

- The LRT is a new rail-based infrastructure. The existing right-of-way of the old train is usable. However, the current system is very slow, is extremely old and cannot take trains with a higher speed. The investment is in a 100% new rail track, new bridges and overpasses (the current system will be dismantled). The LRT will use the same power supply as the existing train system but the rolling stock will be used from the old system. The part of the old train is the right-of-way which of course reduces costs considerably.
- The LRT infrastructure is completely different to that of the old train, with a new rail track, new bridges and overpasses. The LRT will use existing power supply but the rolling stock will be different (new). This means that the LRT will have better quality infrastructure and completely different passenger capacity (20% higher passenger capacity), speeds and connections compared to the old system.
- The LRT replaces existing mixed-train/bus traffic. The Ministry of Transport is currently re-structuring the public transport system in Costa Rica, which includes a new bus network. The Ministry has the authority to re-structure bus routes and concessions. Bus routes parallel to and in the catchment area of the LRT will be closed and restructured. This within a larger Program of restructuring and modernizing the bus systems.
- The LRT is not competing with the existing public transport system, which is the bus network. The public bus system in Costa Rica operates without subsidies to the bus operators i.e., if passenger numbers drop below certain levels the bus operator can close down or give back the concession (this has happened) as they cannot operate the route in a profitable manner.

## Last-mile Connectivity Component

The project will look to long-lived low infrastructure environment including a climate resilient LRT and last-mile connectivity component. The LRT will be constructed in the urban area of the project, while the last-mile connectivity within the catchment area of the project. Impacts will be monitored to establish a solid methodological base to determine the cause-impact relationship and to estimate better the GHS impact of measures (Cruller Consulting, 2020).

## Summary Methodology Input

The project is currently average annual GHG reduction of 173,458 tCO₂e and a lifespan impact of reducing 7,622,805 tCO₂e. This is a conservative calculation as it does not include indirect trip effects of the measures, synergy effects with other projects, or emissions from additional vehicles on also bus routes nor upstream emission effects which would increase the emission reductions claimed.

The program will look to long-lived low infrastructure environment including a climate resilient LRT and last-mile connectivity component. The LRT will be constructed in the urban area of the project, while the last-mile connectivity within the catchment area of the project. Impacts will be monitored to establish a solid methodological base to determine the cause-impact relationship and to estimate better the GHS impact of measures (Cruller Consulting, 2020).

# D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project results in a paradigm shift towards low carbon transport due to the following features:

- The project is integrated with plans to decarbonize further transport through usage of electric vehicles as well as an expansion of public transit and non-motorized transport modes. This will reduce emissions per passenger-km of cycle ways and wants to achieve a shift towards public and NMT plus electrified transport. The project is therefore part of the Costa Rica Green Climate Fund (GCF) proposal, which includes a comprehensive plan that has with the integrated NMT measures a large potential for scaling up and replication throughout the city and other cities in Costa Rica.
- The project is closely integrated with 0-emission last-mile connectivity activities making the LRT a showcase of how to integrate public transport systems with electric vehicles. This will also contribute towards an electrified transport system and with comprehensive last-mile connectivity and NMT interventions can serve as model case for other cities in Costa Rica, Central America, and beyond.
- The project is a step towards electrification of transport. This will result in a massive decrease of emissions per passenger-km due to the nearly fully fossil-free electricity generation of Costa Rica. Transport emissions are at the epicenter of Costa Rica’s climate change mitigation efforts as they represent 20% of total GHG emissions and are not achieving the target of carbon neutrality.
- The project can reduce transport emissions of the GAM by around 85,000 tons CO2e per year in the first phase. This is equivalent to removing more than 19,000 cars from the metropolitan area. This has a significant contribution to a climate-resilient development pathway and to achieving Costa Rica’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC).
- The project includes the collection and reporting of standardized data on the impact of last-mile connectivity interventions in terms of modal shift and reduction of emissions per passenger-km. Results will be published, knowledge products are prepared, and outreach activities are included in the budget for the project to ensure that lessons learned from this project can be shared with other cities learning from experiences and to justify investments based on the expected impacts.
- The project is a key component of the Costa Rica Green Climate Fund (GCF) proposal for an urban public transport system that requires a comprehensive and systemic change. A paradigm shift is made towards offering the original purpose of public transport, which is to provide access to people in their daily lives, work, education, and at a reasonable cost. The transportation sector is transformed into a sustainable low-carbon system with a diverse mix of mobility options.
- The project is line with the post COVID Green Recovery plan of Costa Rica with a focus on resilient infrastructure, economic recovery, and a green economy and helps Costa Rica to achieve its target of decarbonization and net zero emissions by 2050.

# D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)**
The project contributes significantly to sustainable development goals (SDG) 3 (“good health and well-being”), SDG 6 (“clean water and sanitation and infrastructure”), SDG goal 11 (“sustainable cities and communities”), and SDG goal 13 (“climate action”).

|---|---|---|
| PM<sub>10</sub>, reduction | 25 tons | 3,789 tons |

Around 90% of the impact is due to the LRT and the rest due to NMT and last-mile connectivity measures.

## Social Co-Benefits

The project will benefit the GAM with 2.7 million inhabitants through access to safe, reliable, and affordable public transport. In accordance with the World Health Organization's guidelines, the air quality in Costa Rica is considered moderately polluted. The LRT will reduce PM<sub>10</sub>, NO<sub>x</sub>, CO<sub>2</sub> equivalent emissions, and GHG emissions by 35%, which exceeds the recommended maximum of 10 g/m<sup>3</sup>. Improving air quality through zero-combustion emission technology will have a significant positive impact on public health. The LRT will also reduce noise pollution from other air pollutants in the GAM. The LRT has significant health benefits due to improved air quality, less noise pollution and reduced traffic congestion. Improved connectivity components are likely to have a positive impact on these aspects and cycling ways improve the safety of cyclists which aligns with a healthy lifestyle.

## Economic Co-Benefits

Following economic benefits have been identified by the project:

- Time savings valued at 3,154 MUSS (IDOM, 2020)
- Vehicle operating cost savings estimated at $120 MUDD (IDOM, 2020)
- Reduced travel time costs estimated at $68 MUDD (IDOM, 2020)
- Reduced emissions of air pollutants calculated by assigning a monetary value to emissions of PM<sub>10</sub>, NO<sub>x</sub>, and CO<sub>2</sub> equivalent emissions valued at $500 MUSS (Grutter Consulting, 2020)
- Reduced GHG emissions valued based on the social cost of carbon valued at $500 MUSS (Grutter Consulting, 2020)
- Reduced noise emissions valued at $6 MUDD (IDOM, 2020)
- Direct jobs created during operations: 13,849 temporary jobs during construction and 1,460 direct jobs during operations (IDOM, 2020; p. 227/228)

The project has 3.3x more economic benefits than economic costs or an EIR of 26% (IDOM, 2020).

## Gender-Sensitive Development Impact

A gender analysis and a gender action plan have been developed by the project (Grutter Consulting, 2020). The report identifies that women are disproportionately affected by climate change. Women face greater challenges in accessing mobility patterns of women and minimizes the risks of violence. Accessibility ensuring that new infrastructure is in line with requirements of women's participation of women in the transport sector at all stages and decision levels. This project LRT as an NMT infrastructure with the goal of ensuring safe, accessible services catering to the demands and needs of women.

## D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

As with other Mesoamerican Countries, Costa Rica is considered a primary "hot spot" for climate change in the tropics. The country has experienced significant changes in the extreme values of these variables during the period between 1961 and 2003. Extreme precipitation has increased significantly and is strongly correlated with El Niño events. This increase in precipitation is associated with an increase in the frequency of the hydrological cycle, with more intense rain occurring during shorter periods of time that produce greater average precipitation per episode. This trend is expected to continue in the future due to climate change, possibly resulting in

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 38 OF 59

## Country Ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### Country Ownership

Costa Rica has a long tradition in being on the forefront of combating climate change. During the nineties, Costa Rica was one of the first countries to show political awareness on climate change, becoming part of the first pilot implementation projects. In 2007, Costa Rica’s National Climate Change Strategy was launched, supported by the creation of a Climate Change Ministry in 2013. The country has also been a pioneer in the development and implementation of its Decarbonization Plan 2015-2050 which aims for a decarbonized economy with net-zero emissions in 2050 (Costa Rica Decarbonization Plan 2015-2050, MINAE, 2016). The NDCs are aligned to the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS), the Decarbonization Plan 2015-2050 (MINAE, 2016), the NDC (MINAE, 2015), the National Decarbonization Plan (Government of Costa Rica, 2017), the National Energy Policy 2013-2030 (MINAE, 2015) and the National Energy Plan 2015-2030 (MINAE, 2015). The NDC is also aligned to the post COVID-19 Green Recovery Plan of Costa Rica.

The country has a very ambitious target in terms of GHG emissions and 76% of total energy emissions in 2015 (MINAE, 2016). Fostering of public transport and electrifying the transport sector are key strategies to achieve this goal. The NDC includes the electrification of the public transportation system as part of the 2nd BID as well as the NOC of Costa Rica. The NDC includes the train as a mitigation measure and specific actions for urban construction will require both fiscal resources as well as external financial resources.

The country has made significant progress in its electricity sector. In 2019 99.2% of produced electricity was from renewable resources (IEO, 2020). Electrification of the transport sector is a strategy which will reduce drastically the carbon footprint of the country[45].

[45] See annual SERN reports of ICE and for the grid factor (Grutter Consulting, 2020b)

## Engagement with Civil Society Organizations and other Relevant Stakeholders

Since the proposal stage, the Government of Costa Rica has joined forces with the different municipalities that are part of the area of influence of the LRT. December 4th, 2018, the Mayors of the fifteen affected municipalities signed a Framework Agreement with the Municipal Corporations with the aim of developing guidelines to support sustainable and urban development, and the implementation of an integrated urban mobility component complementary to the LRT.

The project will also be implemented in two phases: the first phase is the construction of the LRT and last-mile connectivity investments. The second phase includes multiple rounds of consultations with all stakeholders.

During the construction phase a continuous involvement will be sought. INCOFER as executing entity will have to design and deliver to CABEI citizen participation and consultation plan with quarterly follow-up report of the actions carried out, including the results of the public hearings. CABEI will also develop its own communication plan and consultations with bi-annual monitoring reports by INCOFER or the Concessionaire to CABEI.

## D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The LRT component is co-financed with a GCF loan: 86% is co-finance basically through private sector capital (around 70%) and through a loan from CABEI. The GCF loan has a lower interest rate than the CABEI loan which is expected to be around 3%. This means that the project will have a higher cost recovery ratio for GCF financing and assists in crowding-in of private capital through the PPP.

For the concessionary the LRT is financially profitable – overall, the hurdle rate of 6% will be required. The project has a payback period of less than five years (Gruher Consulting, 2020). The majority of the project due to time savings, positive health and environmental impacts, vehicle operating costs savings and reduced accidentality risk is expected to have an internal rate of return (IRR) greater than 15%. This means that 99% of the capital and 99% of the economic benefits. The main parameters included for the IRR calculation are the cost of capital, discount rate, operating costs, vehicle savings, accidentality risk reduction, economic benefits (%), and reduced acceleration plus producer surplus. The results are robust against a sensitivity analysis as is the profitability threshold which is an important point for passenger demand from where the project is economically not feasible anymore (for example 8%, would be enough demand than projected).

## Table B: Sensitivity of EIRR to Parameter Changes

| Parameter Change | EIRR |
| --- | --- |
| Cost of capital increase by 10% | -25% |
| Discount social rate Costa Rica | +6.3% |
| 50% increase in operating costs | -27% |
| 50% increase OPEX | -25% |
| 50% reduction vehicle savings | -21% |
| 30% reduction of all benefits (i.eom to demand reduction) | -20% |

Calculated with GOM-FSR Economic model, sheet 'Table B'

## NMT, last-mile connectivity & capacity building investments are grant financed by the GCF. This is justified as well as the LRT component because the environmental and social impacts of these initiatives are largely public goods. The magnitude of the benefits can therefore be far less precisely estimated and is predominantly in the medium and long term. The cost recovery ratio for the NMT is expected to be around 15% (Gruher Consulting, 2020). This makes it difficult, especially under the adverse fiscal situation of Costa Rica, where already core investment in the LRT component is challenging. The GCF grant will therefore be used as a risk mitigation measure for the NMT component based on the economic benefits of reduced emissions and reduced vehicle operating costs i.e., for the society these are public goods. This means that 99% of the capital and 100% of the economic benefits.
