benefits are (ART and 1% NMT). The NMT and connect measures in their own are justified with their stand-alone EIRR.

The total capital investment is $1.873 billion USD with a requested GCF finance of 271.3 MUSD and a GHG reduction of 7.62 MtCO₂e, resulting in an internal rate of return (IRR) on the GCF investment of 36 USD/CO₂. GHG marginal abatement cost of transport projects are likely to be higher than other interventions that would have been realized primarily due to the GHG impact but to enable a safe, affordable, efficient, convenient and sustainable public transportation system.

The co-financing rate of the GCF is 5.9 i.e. 86% of the total investment is co-financed. This is a very high rate compared with other transport projects in India.

Technology-wise the project is based on a state-of-the-art LRT. The currently used diesel rail system is completely revamped and replaced with a modern electric LRT system. Modern principles of urban development are applied including mixed-use developments, pedestrian-friendly streets, and public spaces. Smart city measures are also included.

In absence of the GCF financing NMT as well as last-mile connectivity components would not have been included in the project as can be seen from the investment plans of the feasibility reports.

### Assumptions

1. COM Methodology for calculation of LRT (90% ERF, 5% GEF, 5% other) methodology for NMT and 20% methodology for conventional.
2. UNEP: 44 years; based on commercial Microgrid LRT.
3. Total GHG reduction lifespan: 7.625 years (10 years).
4. Average annual GHG reduction: 17.84 t CO2eq.
5. Construction of all lines to be parallel with lines entering into operations within one year at the end of the implementation period i.e., there will be no long-term impact.
6. The final year reflects cumulative reductions by the end of the implementation period.
7. Assumes timely construction and implementation of all lines.
8. Jobs created mid-term are temporary, construction jobs and for line 1.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 3 pages)

In accordance with the AMA the AE will realize an inception Report, a Mile End Evaluation and annual Performance Evaluation plus annual performance reports. INCOFER will provide CABEI project reporting prior to the scheduling of reports to be delivered to the GCF, which will include the monitoring and evaluation plan. The CABEI project team will also prepare the Annual Progress Reports (APRs). After the loan is declared effective, CABEI will hold an inception meeting to discuss the project implementation arrangements, procurement processes, disbursement arrangements, audit, and reporting requirements for the project. CABEI will review progress on a regular basis during the life of the project and provide progress updates and address issues, if needed, to ensure that the project is completed as planned. An independent modern evaluation will be conducted at least 5 years after the loan is declared effective. The achievement or non-attainment of the project's objectives are still likely to be achieved and if changes in the project may be needed. An annual performance report will be prepared by CABEI and submitted to GCF.

INCOFER will submit the draft completion report to CABEI and CABEI will finalize the GCF projec tcompletion report.


E.8. Reporting on lessons learned online


---
**Page 49**
---

b). Impact of the NMT component: The methodological approach to determine the impacts and the monitoring is based on GHG emissions per cycling trip.

c). Impact of the last-mile connectivity component on GHG and local air pollutants: The monitoring methodology is based on control-group approach monitoring the trip emissions per resident of the project area and a control-group and intervention group comparison. This will be carried out in collaboration with the World Bank on a methodology developed by Grutter Consulting the MIT/USA for the World Bank for a project in Nanchang/China.

Reports on the impacts of the LRT, NMT and last-mile connectivity activities will be produced and will be accessible to all stakeholders involved. The monitoring methodologies will also allow for comparison between different intervention measures for an efficient and effective replication of these components. The monitoring methodologies including details can be found in Annex 10.

The cost of the impact monitoring including the specialist surveys and statistical analysis as well as international assistance in this process has been included in the capacity building and monitoring budget financed under a GCF grant to the World Bank. This will allow for the production of reports on the impacts of the LRT, NMT and last-mile connectivity activities not only at project level but also across different project components but also allows to determine better the impact of different intervention measures and to plan further investments in terms of financing and coordination.

3 evaluations are carried out in accordance with Annex 11:

1. Mid-term summative evaluation: This is realized with funds provided under Component 3 by the company contracted for this project. The mid-term evaluation will be conducted at the end of year 2 of the implementation period of the GCF project as of year 3. In years 0 and 1 no infrastructure is yet operational. As such this will represent a progress report and will assess the implementation approach, including the monitoring methodology.

2. Impact evaluation: This is realized with funds provided under Component 3 by the company contracted for this project. The impact evaluation will be conducted at the end of year 5 of the implementation period of the GCF project as of year 6. It will provide an assessment of the overall performance and effectiveness of the project in terms of financing, disbursements, and implementation status, not however to the impact of the project.

3. Final evaluation: This is realized with funds provided under Component 3 by the company contracted for this project. The final evaluation will be conducted at the end of year 7 of the implementation period of the GCF project as of year 8. It will provide an assessment of the overall performance and effectiveness of the project in terms of financing, disbursements, and implementation status, not however to the impact of the project.

The impact monitoring approach is detailed in Annex 11.

E. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

F.1 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures (see p.3 pages)

Selected Risk Factor 1: Right of Way

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

Description

Impossibility of completely regularizing the right-of-way for all lines. This risk concerns mainly the lines 4 and 5, since for the lines 1-3 the line is currently operational with the old train. The impact of this risk would be a delay in project implementation and in the scientific-economic evaluation.

Mitigation Measure(s)

Study and, where appropriate, regularization of the rights that make up the right-of-way. The right-of-way will be based on the technical and economic feasibility studies carried out by the company responsible for the concession. Expropriations will be carried out under the national expropriation regulations (law 7495) and considering the mitigation measures established in this document.

Selected Risk Factor 2: Design Failures

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

Description

Risk that the design fails to achieve the required output capacity, with the result that the project has more or less capacity than necessary to meet the needs of the demand. The impact would be that the infrastructure would be built unprofitably resulting potentially in cost overruns and/or significant potential delays.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The SPV will realize the detailed engineering design. The selection of the private company for this phase is thus critical. A successful project delivery requires a strong technical team with experience in the design of LRTs, full knowledge of the project requirements and how to make its occurrence highly improbable. There are sufficient private companies with experience in designing complex infrastructure projects that have successfully delivered similar projects and similar assets have been designed and implemented successfully in multiple countries worldwide in recent years.

Selected Risk Factor 3: Construction Sur-Cost/Construction Delays

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

Description

Risk that during the design and construction phase the actual project cost exceeds the budgeted costs, due e.g. to an increase in material prices or delays in the delivery of materials. The risk is also high as he agrees to build the LRT infrastructure at an agreed upon CAPEX i.e., any cost overruns not provided through additional funding requested by the government will have to be borne by the private investor. Also construction delays and resultant costs are risks that the private investor has to bear. Successful completed projects in Costa Rica cost-overruns have been in the order of 20%, i.e., the potential impact is estimated at low.

The PPP contract transfers this risk to the private investor. The contract establishes the procedures and penalties in case of implementation delays. This results in PPP contracts in general delivering the project on-time.

Attracting NMT systems including cycling ways and shared mobility services which allow for safe, convenient and efficient access to the destination thus attracting more substantial passenger demand.

## Technical and operational Medium Low

### Category Probability Impact

Higher than expected operational costs resulting in lower than expected profitability and less capacity to repay debts.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The risk is born by the private investor under a PPA contract. Selection of a high-quality private partner is thus key to reducing this risk. Insurance or contractual cover can also be realized for some of the operational cost risks (e.g. technology performance risks).

### Selected Risk Factor 8: Exchange Rate Risk

## Category Probability Impact

This can be related to the payments to the government by the concessionary, in the CAPEX or OPEX of the project.
Exchange rate risks affect the potential revenue in foreign exchange terms and thus the profitability of the project. The type of PPA contract the risk may lie with either the private party, with the government or with both. Results are always a balance between the two parties.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Currency swap contracts, as long as the terms and conditions of such contracts are approved by the contracting institution, so that aggressive positions that go against the government can then and thus an additional risk to the development of the Project.
The currency risk is partly mitigated by the combination of income and expenditure in local currency.

### Selected Risk Factor 9: Inexperienced Execution Entity

## Category Probability Impact

Technical and operational Medium Low

INCOFER is in charge of realizing the concession agreement but lacks experience in that. INCOFER also lacks experience in executing the construction phase, which is both technical and operational phase.
While INCOFER has experience in running its own system, the experience in constructing a new electric line is LIT. This can lead to an increased risk of cost overruns, delays, quality issues, safety incidents, and other risks that could result in non-fulfillment of contractual obligations, non-adquate construction, and construction delays.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

INCOFER receives technical support from CABEI during the concession phase INCOFER will work with the same procedures and approach as used by the National Concessions Council (CCN) and will also establish a project management unit to ensure that the execution of the project is in line with the CCN’s requirements. For the construction and delivery phase CABEI will contract directly 3rd party verification company which will have resources of its own to monitor the quality, safety and progress of the works.
The risk can be mitigated by ensuring proper planning and execution and verify the design products, the global budget, monthly estimates of work, will carry out evaluations of the project’s performance and the quality of the works. CABEI will also ensure that there is a clear understanding on the effect on the time of contractual completion, technical constructive audits, opportune monitoring of change orders, execution of contracts, and follow-up of possible claims or controversies. Likewise it will issue monthly reports to CABEI.

## Selected Risk Factor: 13. Funding gap (availability payment) due to the tight fiscal situation of Costa Rica

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | High |

### Description
The risk is that the government is not capable to finance an availability gap payment to the Co-concessionaire.

### Mitigation measure(s)
- The risk is mitigated by:
  - The fact that the LRT will be operational in 2018, which means that the availability payment will only have to be made once the LRT is operational (i.e. 6 years from now). The measures to reduce the fiscal deficit have already been taken since December 2015 and will continue to be implemented until at least June 2017 (and possibly beyond, as additional measures may have again been taken by end 2015) and will have an impact in a much shorter period. Therefore the future risk is considerably reduced.
- With the closure of the Concession contract the Costa Rican government assumes the legal obligation of the availability payment. The cost of this availability payment would be approximately 3% of the total project cost, which means that the availability payment would have meant 0.5% of the Costa Rican budget i.e. whilst significant this is not a high fiscal risk.
- Investing in profitable infrastructure will alleviate future fiscal problems. The reduced health costs due to reduced mortality and morbidity (which are expected to be paid for by the government through the public health system) will have an impact on the fiscal situation of the country, which will result in increased productivity of the economy which again results in increased tax revenues. Overall the fiscal effect is that this availability payment will not have a significant negative impact.

The GCF financing enables to reduce this risk strongly by having a long loan tenure allowing to spread out payments and by providing a financial mechanism that the fiscal situation is not aggravated of Costa Rica whilst enabling the investment in green infrastructure.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.6 pages)

### Environmental Impacts

A separation of potential environmental impacts during the construction and operational phase is made. Major potential environmental impacts of the project during construction are related to traffic disruptions and access to properties; relocation of utilities, cutting trees, noise and air quality impacts, as well as community health and safety risks associated with the presence of heavy machinery in residential areas. The project will specifically impact on urban air quality and acoustic environment. Adequate mitigation measures are incorporated into project design and construction plans. A detailed environmental impact assessment (EIA) was conducted to identify various stakeholders were consulted? The preliminary EIA was disclosed on INCOFIN's website June 2020 (DOIM, 2020, p. 193). The final EIA was approved by the Board of Directors on August 27th and entered October 24th 2019 with approval of the loan through the Board of CAB on October 30th 2019.

The final EIA will be read by the concessionary. The train requires an environmental permit and authorization granted by the National Environmental Technical Secretariat (SETENA), which involves compliance with relevant regulations, including those related to noise pollution, air quality, and waste management (DOIM, 2020, p. 191 to 216). For construction the concessionary will require permits from the Ministry of Public Works and Transportation (MOPVT) and the corresponding municipalities. The operational permit is given by MOPVT.

### Social Impacts

The project will affect and potentially displace around 215 people who have invaded the right way of the train system in the last years (DOIM, 2020, p. 143). The negotiation mechanisms for the process of expropriation and resettlement are described in detail in the EIA. In addition to the compensation provided by the concessionary, processes will be followed to compensate the persons or companies that own property where some new sections of the railway line will be built (DOIM, 2020, p. 143). The project will affect approximately 50 families and the total expropriation area is estimated at 420,000 m² and 28,000 m³ of buildings (DOIM, 2020). INCOFIN must deliver a resettlement plan to the Ministry of Public Works and Transportation within six months after obtaining land acquisition and expropriations, and corresponding protocols. At least five months after the last resettlement, the concessionary will submit an evaluation report on the implementation of the resettlement plan considering the reestablishment or improvement of socioeconomic indicators and conditions of affected families.

The project is located in the urban area of San José, where no indigenous peoples reside. The project will not affect any indigenous communities and, accordingly, no indigenous peoples planning documents are required (DOIM, 2020, p. 147).

The project will benefit GAMA estimation population of 2.7 million through increased access to safe, reliable, and affordable public transport. Overall, the project is expected to generate 1,200 temporary jobs during construction and 1,190 permanent jobs in operation (DOIM, 2020, p. 35). The project will also contribute to reducing CO2 emissions by 272/227tCO2e. The affordability of the services is analyzed closely. The new fare schedule will largely follow the existing levels (DOIM, 2020, p. 148). The project is expected to reduce traffic congestion and air pollution which reduces mortality and morbidity rates through improved air quality, reduced noise emissions and reduced accidentality.

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Gender Assessment<sup>16</sup>
Costa Rica has been committed to closing gender gaps for almost a half a century. Section that are less traditional for women are more likely to have significant gender gaps. In Costa Rica, nearly 60% of public transport users are women, yet there are very few women in decision-making positions or as employees in the sector, and only 10% of bus drivers are women. Women also have access to other vehicles (only 30% of licenses have been issued to women). This is a greater dependence on public transport for women.

The 2004 specialized survey on violence against women shows that 58% of the women surveyed reported having been the victim of at least one incident of physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives since the age of 16. The same survey also found that 37% of women had experienced sexual harassment, and 20% of women inhibits them from accessing public transport services and other services provided by use of buses, trains or taxis because they constantly experience sexual harassment, sexual violence and other types of violence while they are users.

Based on the analysis of available information, the transport sector in Costa Rica continues to be dominated by men for all positions. The Ministry of Transport and Communications (MTC) has a 100% male executive board. Currently, INCOFER has a woman as its executive president. Within the institution's board of directors, 43% (2 of 7 positions), including the executive president of the positions were awarded to women and 58% or more (4 of 7 positions).

Gender Action Plan
The gender action plan seeks to incorporate activities that will improve women’s participation as users and providers of public transport services. Dimensions included in the gender action plan are: i) mobility and safety; ii) accessibility and iii) participation. Within mobility and safety it is proposed to design activities that promote the transportation of women, especially those who are most vulnerable (e.g., pregnant women). The aim is to provide information on how to avoid violence against women and to men in order to prevent and attend to violence against women. Also, information shall be generated on women’s mobility patterns (accompanied by shopping carts, multiple bags or packages, or挣扎者 for children). In terms of accessibility it is necessary to consider the design of public transport services that are adapted to women's specific mobility patterns (accompanied with shopping carts, multiple bags or packages,挣扎者 for children). In terms of participation, it will be necessary to ensure that women participate in decision-making at all levels: from the inside the stations, but also the connection with other types of multimodal transport and other public spaces. The emphasis shall be on ensuring that women are involved in the design of services and infrastructure, as well as in the mobility patterns (accompanied by shopping carts, multiple bags or packages,挣扎者 for children). In terms of participation it will be necessary to ensure that women participate at all levels: from inside the stations, but also the connection with other types of multimodal transport and other public spaces. The emphasis shall be on ensuring that women are involved in the design of services and infrastructure, as well as in the mobility patterns (accompanied by shopping carts, multiple bags or packages,挣扎者 for children).

CABEI will engage a third party verification company paid with resources of the borrower (2.4% of the loan amount), with field presence. The follow-up will be carried out through the reports issued by the verifying firm, and through review and field visits, according to the annual plan. The verification of the environmental and social aspects per line will be carried out by the Bank's Office of Environmental and Social Sustainability (OFAS), who will accompany the UCSO, according to the frequency established in the annual supervision plan.

San José, March 4th, 2021
DM-206-3021

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

Re: Funding proposed for the GCF by the Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CAEI) regarding Light Rail Transit for the Metropolitan Area of San José (GAM) in Costa Rica as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Central American Bank for Economic Integration to the Green Climate Fund on March 4th, 2021.

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project Light Rail Transit for the Metropolitan Area of San José (GAM) in Costa Rica as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Central American Bank for Economic Integration to the Green Climate Fund on March 4th, 2021.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Environment and Energy, the National Designated Authority of Costa Rica.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.6(0)15, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby confirm that:

(a) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Costa Rica's national priorities, strategies, and plans;

(b) In accordance with relevant environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for accounting non-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

# Secretariat's assessment of FP166

## Proposal name:
Light Rail Transit for the Greater Metropolitan Area (GAM)

## Accredited entity:
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

## Country/ies:
Costa Rica

## Project/programme size:
Large

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The project has a relatively low greenhouse gas (GHG) impact due to electrification powered by >98 per cent renewable resources.
- The project expects to play a key role in effecting systematic change in public transport, integrating non-motorized and other transport modes including buses, routes of which will be reorganized around the light rail transit system.

#### Points of caution
- A continuous fiscal burden is expected by the government for the subsidy payment to the project during the operation of the LRT.
- The selection of a concessionaire that meets both technical and financial requirements is critical given the high upfront capital investment required from the private sector.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum VI, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations," respectively.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

#### 2.1 Project background
- The project aims to establish a modern, fast, safe and environmentally sound electric light rail transit (LRT) system with 85 kilometers (km) of double tracks on five lines, which will be integrated with non-motorized transport (NMT) and transit-oriented development measures.


---
**Page 70**
---

# LRT System (Total cost: USD 1,452 million; GCF cost: USD 250 million)

This component seeks to finance the infrastructure for the LRT, including:

(a) Infrastructure and systems worth USD 834 million with the main elements being rail tracks, stations, rolling stock, signaling and communication systems and the rehabilitation of affected services. This subsequent also includes the construction of a new station in San José and the rehabilitation of historic patrimony and USD 4 million for waste management;

(b) Rolling stock worth USD 512 million for 78 trains at USD 6.0 million per unit;


---
**Page 71**
---

## Financing changes during implementation estimated at USD 298 million

### Other costs of USD 260 million including acquisition of land and building, design and implementation, and indirect costs (administration, profit, contingency). This subcomponent also includes USD 10 million or environmental and social measures.

The project will be implemented through the creation of a new public transport system for decades, including restructuring the existing bus system, which is dominated by more than 40 private bus operators. The integrated sustainable mobility plan for GMA in Costa Rica (integrated LRT system) has been developed to address this situation and concludes that an integrated transport system with an LRT combined with a bus system based on a revenue-based fare structure will be the most appropriate solution. The project expects that once the LRT system is established, the competing bus routes will be redirected to serve as feeder services for the LRT from which they can then be integrated into an integrated fare system.

15. While the LRT is deemed a more attractive option in terms of both (i) performance on speed and capacity, and (ii) its GHG emission reductions, it often entails high capital expenditures not only for civil works and rolling stock but also for land acquisition. The project proposes to finance these costs through a public-private partnership (PPP) model for a diesel train. This will reduce the investment cost of the project by 50 per cent compared to similar LRT projects in other countries, which are typically financed using government resources, while providing more reliable, consistent and sustainable transport service to the community with a longer lifespan of the asset.

16. PPPs are generally structured as a PPP transaction and requires a concessionaire to cover the initial capital investment up to the operation of each of the five lines to build the structure. The project proposes that the concessionaire be a public-private partnership (PPP) agreement with an SPV that is 100 per cent owned by the private sector and takes into consideration project attractiveness to private sector investors, country context and risk allocation among different parties. The concessionaire will be responsible for financing all costs associated with the LRT system commences operation, and the proceeds will go to project cashflow and ultimately to the government as a return on investment during the concession period in the concession phase. The proposed implementation structure is provided in Figure 1.

17. The payment scheme is not yet defined but is subject to negotiation between the government and the concessionaire. It may include (i) a performance-based demand payment (Pago por Disponibilidad y transferencia de ingresos (PDP)) with an end-demand risk; and (ii) availability payments (Pago por disponibilidad (PPD)).

18. The government expects to pay around USD 96-123 million for the respective concession period, which is expected to be paid in two instalments: (i) a technical tariff (agreed under the concession contract) and the passenger tariff (which is set at a level usual for public transport services); and (ii) an availability payment based on the minimum rate of return (RR). Under the PPDOR scenario, a higher equity RR of 13 per cent is expected compared that of the PPP model, as the SPV would bear the demand risk to a certain extent.

19. The project aims to achieve an emission reduction of 65 million CO2eq through the modal shift from passenger cars (units including ride-sharing services and existing bus systems) to LRT-based public transport.


---
**Page 72**
---

# Figure 1. Public-private partnership implementation structure

| Component 2: Urban integration with NMT/accessibility/availability (total cost: USD 20 million; GCF cost: USD 18 million) |
|---|
| The project will deliver: |
| * Sixteen km of segregated cycle lanes connecting the LRT stations and parking stations with optimal lighting and safety measures; and |
| * Infrastructure required to improve the connectivity/accessibility of the LRT system at existing transit nodes, including a multi-modal integration with other modes of transport such as bus systems, and various access bridges for pedestrians and bicycles. The project will also introduce green spaces and promote the greening of spaces. |

The GAM currently has less than 20 km of non-integrated cycle lanes. The country has limited experience with NMT and transit-oriented development (TOD)/community measures and its linkage with the mass transit system while not taking advantage of the potential for TOD to improve land use, increase accessibility, and attract different donor agencies and funding from the GCF Project Preparation Facility, the county accredited institution, and other sources. The project will introduce green spaces and introduced such interventions to this project.

The improved safety and interconnection with the LRT system, including bike parking spaces, will reduce the number of trips made by pedestrians between stations for transfers for pedestrians and improved connectivity for entire trips without complex transfers to other modes of transport. This will contribute towards a more comprehensive transformation of the public transport landscape of the project area.

Through behavioral change and a contribution to the modal shift toward public transport, the project aims at a cumulative emissions reduction of 1.08 million tCO2e by the end of the project lifetime.

Component 3: Capacity-building and gender measures (total cost USD 1.3 million; GCF cost USD 1.2 million).

**Page 73**
---

### Impact potential

**3.1 Impact potential**

* **Scale:** High

In total, 7.62 million tCO₂eq are expected to be mitigated over the lifespan of the project. An estimated 80 per cent of the emission reductions due to the LRT can be attributed to the fact that the LRT runs on electricity and is powered by a battery system. The remaining 20 per cent of the GHG reductions from the project are attributed to the fostering of cycling and mixed mobility, and 10 per cent is due to NMT interventions. While the figure appears smaller compared with other transport modes such as buses or cars, it should be noted that in Costa Rica, where the project will take place, emissions from agriculture and forestry, the impact potential is on the high end for typical transport projects thanks to the low emission factor of electricity in Costa Rica.

**3.2 Paradigm shift potential**

* **Scale:** High

GCF funding is critical for the country to overcome financial barriers and limited experience with NMT that hinder the development of a modern, multi-modal low-carbon public transport system.


---
**Page 74**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

The LRT is a core component of the post-COVID-19 Green Recovery Plan of Costa Rica.

The project is expected to contribute significantly to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and National Development Plans, including SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities) and SDG 13 (climate action).

From the environmental perspective, the operation of LRT combined with SMF will result in a reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. In addition to these co-benefits, the project is expected to generate 1,200 temporary jobs during construction and 1,460 direct jobs during operations. The total economic benefits are estimated at US$358 million, which can be monetized to a total amount of USD 5,626 million. While all of these factors would be important, only good financial performance through low pollution and climate change impacts is included in the economic analysis. The project has an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 26 per cent.

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: Medium to high**

Costa Rica's net zero goal would require a decarbonized transport sector that currently stands as the top GHG emitter of the country. The needs of the population to access a cleaner and more efficient form of transport are also fewer private vehicles are well-established. The concessionaire is expected to be financially viable during the concession period, which is 30 years.

### Country ownership

**Scale: High**

The country ownership structure of this project as announced in high. Costa Rica has been ambitious in its climate goals and it is among a decarbonized economy with net zero emissions by 2050. The transport sector is the largest GHG emitter in Costa Rica; it contributes 31 per cent of the country's total greenhouse gas emissions. Decarbonization of this sector has been included in various national development plans and strategies, including its nationally determined contribution, National Decarbonization Plan 2018–2050, and the National Transport Plan for Costa Rica 2019-2034. The government is committed to subsidize at a rate of 60 per cent to make the LRT financially profitable despite its fiscal constraints.

The proposal has been developed in consultation with relevant stakeholders, including government agencies, development partners, academia and civil society. In addition, the project has a strong institutional framework for implementation during and after the construction phase.


---
**Page 75**
---

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The efficiency and effectiveness potential of this proposal is considered high.

### Environmental and social safeguards

#### Project background
The project involves upgrading the existing single-track diesel passenger train system into a state-of-the-art, multi-track dual-voltage electric passenger train system. The project consists of three components: (i) an LRT system; (ii) urban integration measures; and (iii) environmental and social safeguard measures.

#### Environmental and social risk category
The project has been assigned Category A since it represents a significant increase in the number of people exposed to risks that, individually or cumulatively, are diverse, irreversible, or unprecedented. There are also potential adverse impacts on biodiversity due to land acquisition and the possible displacement of dwellings, structures, and business establishments.

#### Safeguards instruments
A PES was submitted by the AE, which provided an initial assessment of the potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the project.


---
**Page 76**
---

formal environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) will have to be prepared by the future concessionaire when the detailed engineering design has been specified. The ESIA will be the basis for the issuance of the environmental license by the National Environmental Technical Secretariat (NETS). The ESIA will also serve as a reference document in the Environmental, Emission and Mitigation of Environmental and Social Risks, its tool for identifying potential environmental and social risks. In parallel with the preparation of the ESIA, an Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) was developed and submitted. Since the detailed ESIA cannot be undertaken yet (pending the engagement of the concessionaire), which will undertake the detailed engineering design, a separate Environmental, Emission and Social Management framework (ESMF) that provides guidance on undertaking environmental and social management activities during the detailed engineering design preparation.

Status of compliance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards. The following document presents the status of the project's compliance with GCF ESS standards:

(a) ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social impacts and risks. A preliminary ESIA has been prepared by the future concessionaire, which includes the ESAP and an ESMF were prepared. The ESMF has specified the scope and outlined the processes and steps for the conduct of the detailed ESIA simultaneously with the detailed engineering design preparation. The ESIA will include all environmental and social assessments of the country, the AEU and the GCF. The ESIA will include, among other things, the baseline conditions, the project description, the identification of potential adverse impacts, induced, long-term and cumulative impacts including from associated facilities, and the measures to avoid or mitigate these impacts as well as the measures to monitor and compensate for residual impacts. Frameworks for stakeholder engagement and the establishment of grievance mechanisms will be included.

(b) ESS 2: Labour and working conditions. Based on the ESIA, the country's legal framework on labour and working conditions is fully in par with the International Labour Organization (ILO). The ESIA has identified a number of risks related to non-compliance from contractors and subcontractors as regards health and safety of workers during construction activities. Nevertheless, the ESMF recommends the following measures: (a) prohibition of child labour; (b) prohibition of forced labour; (c) strict observance of labour standards; (d) provision of labour management guidelines to contractors and subcontractors on the implementation of ILO conventions; (e) quality of labour and working conditions by project management. The risk of non-compliance and the additional measures are fully addressed in the ESMF.

(c) ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution control and prevention. There are sufficient laws in the country on the abstraction and industrial use of water. The use of water for construction activities is subject to a permit system, which is fully in compliance with the regulation is low. In terms of pollution, the project will likely involve the generation of fumes and dust during demolition works as well as the removal of concrete structures and demolition of existing concrete structures. The ESMF provides a list of measures to minimize or mitigate these impacts on air quality. These will also be validated and further refined in the ESIA.

(d) ESS 4: Community health, safety and security. The risks involved under this standard include those associated with the potential for increased crime rates due to construction activities as well as the risks associated with the influx of labour and economic migrants into their communities. These risks may be exacerbated by the project's potential to increase in criminality and gender-based violence, including sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment risks and cultural conflicts). The ESMF suggests requiring contractors to provide security measures for workers on site; prohibit smoking within 50 meters of major construction areas; install barriers and proper signage; and provide safe and well-


---
**Page 77**
---

highway alignments and road crossings for the public. These risks and measures will also be further assessed and revised in the ESIA.

Earthquake hazard is also particularly significant in Costa Rica for large public infrastructure due to its tectonic profile. Fire hazards have also been historically significant, especially at the stations. The project area is located within a seismic zone. The ESMP suggests strict compliance with the National Fire Protection Association codes in the design and construction of buildings, bridges, and other structures. This will ensure code and good international industry practice in the structural design of buildings, bridges and demolition areas.

### S5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project will involve right-of-way recovery and additional land acquisition for the expansion of tracks, stations and the construction of new infrastructure around the stations. Initial estimates show that the project will affect and potentially displace approximately 100 families in the communities surrounding the project areas.

To address the potential involuntary resettlement impacts of these land acquisitions, the ESMP includes a resettlement policy framework that requires and will guide the preparation of an Environmental Social Action Plan (ESAP) for the affected communities including land acquisition and expropriation, economic restoration and land donations (as applicable).

### S6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

There are no officially declared protected wilderness areas within the influence area. However, there is a significant amount of biodiversity in the Guanacaste Protection Zone. However, the existing rail lines passes through patches or sometimes long corridors of forested land that provide habitat for many species.

The project will also affect and potentially displace approximately 100 families in the communities surrounding the project areas.

To address the potential involuntary resettlement impacts of these land acquisitions, the ESMP includes a resettlement policy framework that requires and will guide the preparation of an Environmental Social Action Plan (ESAP) for the affected communities including land acquisition and expropriation, economic restoration and land donations (as applicable).

# Gender policy

## Assessment of gender equality

The GCF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

### The gender assessment was conducted using desk reviews as well as through consultations with key stakeholders, including women's organizations at national, regional, and local levels; women's organizations and groups, as well as private sector institutions and civil society organizations (CSOs). The assessment demonstrated that the proposed environmental project will have a significant impact on women in Costa Rica. Costa Rica has regulatory frameworks and a national policy for effective equality between women and men, which include: [i] legal measures to ensure equal access of women and men to employment, health and education; [ii] strategic action on equal rights time and wage parity during the implementation of public policies; and [iii] gender mainstreaming in various sectors including public transport and climate change. Despite the existence of an enabling environment for gender equality, women are still underrepresented in decision-making processes and positions at all levels; women do not enjoy significant representation as managers or leaders in the private sector. Furthermore, it is noted that around 60 per cent of women are users of public transport yet there are only a few women in decision-making positions and as employees in the sector. Ten per cent of bus driver positions are held by women, while 15 per cent of bus conductors are female. Women tend to use public transport more than men for commuting purposes; however, they also make more trips and caregiving tasks, trips are usually short in terms of time and distance. Few women have driver's licenses or access to private vehicles due to financial constraints and lack of education. The reasons for this are not understood yet. Inferences from other contexts indicate that women choose to use certain modes of transport based on the level of convenience between stations,


---
**Page 79**
---

frequency of travel and safety inside trains and stations. Given such considerations, women use cabs, buses, other and bicycles much more than men. The enumerations indicate that overall; there is insufficient information to understand the differentiated preferences and needs of women in transport.

The assessment further notes that (i) the unequal access and position of women is perpetuated by norms and practices that dictate that women should work only if their partner's salary allows it, which results in a gendered division of labor. This contributes to inequality in access to opportunities and decision-making. Women in Costa Rica spend more time on unpaid domestic work than men, which limits their ability to participate fully in the economy. This also demands the greatest investment of women’s time. This results in women having less time to find and keep a job. It is also noted that COVID-19 has resulted in an increase in domestic violence as a result of women losing jobs or having to work from home, which they feel it seems to be in low productivity sectors; and (ii) women have their jobs to take on more household-related responsibilities than men do. This results in women having less time for education and training opportunities across all sectors and for all types of jobs in both the public and private sectors. While employment, wage gaps are also a concern as women earn 20% less than men in Costa Rica. In addition to this, at the same time the public transport sector is where women face significant levels of violence as they commute daily. The lack of information on the magnitude of the problem makes it difficult and substantive the magnitude of the problem and not enough information about women’s safety and security in the transport sector. There are also no specific policies or measures put in place to raise awareness about the implications of sexual harassment prevalent in such services.

The AE has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the project. The document provides an overview of the challenges faced by women in the transport sector and includes indicators and timelines for the implementation of planned activities. The gender action plan is structured around three main themes and targets are presented in a sex-disaggregated manner. All activities have timelines set at year 5 with corresponding budgets. The National Institute for Women (INAMU), as the expert institution, will be responsible for monitoring and evaluating progress on the gender action plan in the implementation of planned activities outlined in the gender action plan in close collaboration with other institutions.

Based on the gender assessment and challenges identified particularly in the transport sector, the project has developed the gender action plan with components and activities that aim to address these challenges. The gender action plan is structured around three main themes as both transport service users and providers. The activities included in the gender action plan are: (i) raising awareness on women’s safety and security; (ii) improving access to transportation mobility patterns and preferences through surveys and evaluations; providing non-motorized mobility options for women, particularly those with disabilities or who have limited mobility; and (iii) through the development of applications for rapid reporting systems and smartphones that allow for reporting violence against women, and ensuring women’s access to services through gender-sensitive service delivery. The project will also provide training on equal opportunities jobs for women in the public sector, especially in trains, while at the same time creating an enabling environment for women to engage in all decision-making roles.

### Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute (medium risk)

- The AE, CABEI, is the regional development entity and has experience investing in the region over five decades.
- From 2010 to 2018, CABEI projects totalled USD 13 billion of which USD 5.7 billion was for infrastructure projects in Costa Rica.
- The project is within the same region; these investments, although of relatively smaller size, have given CABEI experience with similar projects.

- The EE is Incofer, which is a state-owned rail authority that operates the country's railway network.
- A capacity assessment of Incofer conducted by CABEI concluded that the institution has limited experience in running a new LRT system and that while it does have experience in running its rail system, its experience in constructing a new electric LRT is limited. The EE will require support from CAB to carry out the tasks.

### Project-specific risks (high risk)

- **Funding gap:** The project has a large with an expected cost of USD 1,673 million, of which the sources for 70 per cent financing are not yet identified and will be finalized only after the award of the concession to the private concessionaire. It may not be possible to award the concession before the end of the year.
- **Bankability of the concession:** Currently, the terms of the concession agreement are not available; therefore, it is not clear what the financial structure of the concession will look like and whether the concessionaire and the government is yet to be determined. In addition, potential changes in the tariff and its influence on the PPP contract have not yet been decided.
- **Electricity supply and pricing:** The terms of the concession agreement are not available; therefore, it is not clear what the electricity supply and pricing will look like for the concessionaire. The AE has indicated that it will not manage, supervise or audit the concessionaire's performance in relation to the electricity supply and pricing.
- **Project preparation phase (PPP transaction):** The terms of the concession agreement are not available; therefore, it is not clear what the project preparation phase (PPP transaction) will look like. The EE will assist in the PPP project design and in structuring the PPP transaction. An adequate tariff will need to be established that reflects the cost of providing a reliable service at competitive concessionaire stable rates, currency risk management and sound structuring are necessary to improve the bankability.
- **Impact risk:** The AE has estimated the project's mitigation impact at 7.6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e) over the project lifetime of 44 years. However, as per the original project document, the mitigation impact was estimated at 10 MTCO2e and not 7.6 MTCO2e (1.4 per cent of the total present impact) as the AE will monitor and report to GCF on the complete post-impact of the project.
- **Effective use of GCF funds in case of delay:** Approve: 92 per cent of the GCF disbursement is projected to be disbursed within two years. The AE has indicated that it will present a bill to Congress of Costa Rica to start the tender process for the PPP. The credit for financing the state contribution to the concession (financed through CAIIE) is being discussed in Congress, and the

funding remains but has not provided any target date. The GCF has not yet received any formal request for funding from the Government. After the term sheet includes a covenant that the concessionaire to be selected by the government shall achieve financial close within 12 months after the signing of the term sheet, or in case of the subsidiary agreement. For the model where the concessionaire takes part in the demand risk, an FIRR of 13.5 per cent and an NPV of US$20 million are estimated. If the concessionaire does not take up the government, an FIRR of 10 per cent is estimated. All aspects that the project will be able to recover 65 per cent of operational costs or 40 per cent of total costs through tariffs. Therefore, the government's continued ability to subsidize the project has a significant impact on its financial viability.

56. Credit risk: GCF is providing a sovereign loan to the Government of Costa Rica (Moody’s B2). The credit profile of the country remains weak due to increased funding risks as a result of pension reform and the need for additional public spending in 2019. However, the project has a long-term repayment profile which is linked to the balance sheet of GCF and reduces the financial risk.

57. Concessionality: GCF is requested to provide a highly concessional loan to the Government of Costa Rica. This concessionality is expected to result in a lower tariff for the end users. However, this will increase the project cost as it will reduce the difference between the project cost and the cost of other financing. The project cost includes a financing cost of USD 298 million, which represents an interest rate of 10 per cent over 25 years. The GCF Board has provided a prudent check on the project cost and co-financing pricing as part of its due diligence in the implementation of the project.

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

The AE of this project, CABEI shall administer the GCF proceeds to be disbursed for the implementation of the project. CABEI will make at least two supervisory visits per year.

The SPV is Incoter. A capacity assessment of Incoter was conducted by CABEI. The assessment concluded that the institution is in full capacity to oversee the concession contract for the LRT and NMT. The use of GCF proceeds is for refinancing the bridge loan. CABEI and GCF resources are disbursed based on the constructed LRT lines of the concessionaire. The sovereign loans given by GCF and CABEI to the Government of Costa Rica will be used as a counterpart payment for the concession contract. As such, CABEI does not manage, supervise or audit the concession execution.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP166

## Proposal name: Light Rail Transit for the Greater Metropolitan Area (GAM)

### Accredited entity: Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

### Country/programme size: Costa Rica / Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Project description
This is a transport infrastructure project, which will be implemented along the central east-west axis of the larger urban area of San José, Costa Rica, known as Gran Área Metropolitana (GAM). The transport infrastructure will be developed as a light rail transit (LRT) system with 85 km of double tracks on five lines. The LRT is expected to transport annually about 63 million passengers and reduce the number of private cars by 10,427 per year in the GAM area.

#### 1.2 Project context
The project will be implemented within the framework of the Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) and will contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions from transport by replacing fossil fuel-based public transit with a low-carbon electric rail system. The LRT is expected to reduce CO2 emissions by 10,427 metric tons per year.

#### 1.3 Project objectives
The project aims to improve the quality of life in the GAM area and contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions from transport by replacing fossil fuel-based public transit with a low-carbon electric rail system. The LRT is expected to reduce CO2 emissions by 10,427 metric tons per year.

#### 1.4 Project implementation
The project will be implemented within the framework of the Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) and will contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions from transport by replacing fossil fuel-based public transit with a low-carbon electric rail system. The LRT is expected to reduce CO2 emissions by 10,427 metric tons per year.

#### 1.5 Project monitoring
The project will be monitored using the baseline data and the monitoring methodology for LRT and bus rapid transit (BRT) projects have been adopted for the current project.


---
**Page 86**
---

1. When the LRT is built and commissioned, it is expected that some of the passengers who use the baseline modes listed above will shift to the use of the modern, fast and convenient LRT as the single-line rail will be decommissioned and replaced with the LRT. It is also stated that all the public bus routes in the area served by the LRT will be restructured immediately after the LRT is operational. Passengers using the baseline modes along routes parallel to the LRT may shift to use of the LRT as well, but this is not expected to happen on a large scale.
2. There are indications that at the start of the LRT operation, modal shift from the public bus system to the LRT may be limited and that the two systems will continue to operate in parallel for some time until interconnections are made. Each operator has a concession for their line and all base lines diverge into the City Centre, where passengers can change to another bus line or get to their destination. Bus lines are not interconnected at the City Centre either.
3. The restructuring of the public (Diquis) bus routes, aimed at regenerating seamless travel between the new LRT and the public buses, is currently being carried out. The output of the ongoing route restructuring exercise is expected to be available fairly soon after the completion of the survey. It should be noted that the restructured bus routes will not include any existing bus-bus and bus-LRT connections.
4. Since the bus routes are owned by private operators, the public transport authority (TAF) has no control over how these routes are operated, and the restructuring of the remaining bus routes must be carried out in such a way that it will avoid public protest. The survey results show that the majority of the respondents believe that other bus operators in Costa Rica are usually locked in a competitive position, disagree on common approaches to route planning and have no control over how the restructuring will be implemented.
5. As no information was available regarding the modal shift from the baseline modes to the LRT, it is understood that owners of the routes who will be restructured will not be able to use the same ticketing system as the LRT. The TAF has stated that the implementation of a single integrated electronic fare system that would allow the use of the same ticket when switching modalities between the bus and the LRT, as well as between the bus and the train (Trenes y Transporte), are outside the scope of the proposed project.
6. Therefore, some of the modal shift from the baseline modes to the LRT may be limited by the fact that the electronic fare system will not be implemented in the near future. The survey results show that the majority of respondents captured by the e-survey assume some of the linkages (bus-bus and bus-LRT) will not exist before the LRT is operational, which means that the modal shift from baseline modes to the LRT may be lower than expected.
7. The fractional shares of the modal shifts may appear to be lower than they will be in real terms, leading to an underestimation of the implication of modal shift. Since this will deliver baseline GgC emissions, it can be concluded that the adoption of different emission reduction will be conservative.
8. The project relies on the assumption that the LRT, the SMT and bus mode connectivity activities. A brief description of each of the emission reduction pathways is described in Box 4 below.


### GREEN CLIMATE FUND
#### PROJECT IMPLEMENTATION PATHWAYS FOR THE LRT PROJECT


## GHG EMISSION REDUCTION PATHWAYS FOR THE LRT PROJECT

### GHG emission reduction

GHG emissions are reduced by the LRT through modal shift. A key GHG emission reduction estimate is the number of passengers who will switch from baseline modes to use of the transport mode that travelers using the LRT would have used if the project activities were not implemented (i.e., in the absence of the LRT). The number of passengers and the average annual year of the LRT will be determined by sample surveys, where travelers on the LRT will be questioned as to how they may have made their previous journey (from the baseline modes) had there been no LRT mode. The modal shift is then calculated based on the number of LRT passengers (including the type, fuel used in the baseline, average travel times on each mode in the baseline and the average annual year). The modal shift will be determined by using the most up-to-date local data being used for the baseline calculation. In addition, using in-country data through a Well-To-Wheel (WTW) analysis, emissions per ton (tCO2e) for each of the modes present


---
**Page 87**
---

# GHG Emissions WTW per km on passenger modes in GAM, 2019 (gCO2eq/km)

| Mode | Passenger |
| --- | --- |
| Taxi | 350 |
| Motorbike | 70 |
| Bus | 40 |
| Existing train | 60 |
| LRT | 8 |

## Abbreviations: WTW = Well-to-Wheel; pkm = passenger-kilometer.

Source: [Gerster Consulting, 2020b]1; all emissions factors based on localized data.

### (i) Baseline GHG emissions

Baseline emissions are then calculated based on the number of passengers, the mode share, the EF per passenger and average trip distance. The EF per passenger is:

BE = p × Σ(MSR, TDP), KF, XP

Where:
- BE: Baseline emissions (tCO2eq)
- p: Number of passengers
- MSR: Share of LRT passengers who would have used the LRT in a particular year
- TDP: Share of LRT passengers who would have used the bus instead of the LRT that year; project emission = electricity consumed by the LRT minus the amount of CO2eq emitted by the bus
- KF: EF of mode (gCO2eq/pkm)
- XP: To determine the emissions factor of PM2.5, nitrogen oxides and sulfur dioxide the same equation is used but with the EFp for the respective pollutant.

### (ii) Project GHG emissions

Only direct project impacts are taken into account for its calculation. The same is applied for the baseline emissions except that the project is taken in and not the entire trip distance from origin to destination. Project emissions are based on electricity consumption of the LRT and the carbon factor of the electricity grid of Costa Rica.

PE = EC × KF,EF20

Where:
- PE: Project emissions (tCO2eq)
- EC: Electricity consumption of the LRT (MWh)
- KF,EF20: Carbon factor of the electricity grid of Costa Rica


---
**Page 88**
---

Emission reductions are baseline emissions minus project emissions.

d = (p - r)

Where:

* d: Emission reductions (tCO2eq)
* p: Baseline emissions (tCO2eq)
* r: Project emissions (tCO2eq)

The same approach is used for GHG emissions as well. For the estimation of the emission of local pollutants with the project, if the project has no direct local pollutants, that is, project emissions of local pollutants is zero.

### 6. SMF component composition

This section describes the approach to determine the GHG impact based on a Global Environment Facility approach (GEF, 2015) based on (i) cycle of lines; (ii) number of additional cycling trips; and (iii) average distance traveled per trip. The default values are those of baseline modes. Global Environment Facility default values and monitored local data are used.

#### 6. Last-mile connectivity component composition

The last-mile connectivity component class was developed by Gutter Consulting and the Massachusetts Institute of Technology for the World Bank and was applied in a project in Nanjing, China. The methodology is based on the assumption that the baseline scenario includes transportation GHG emissions per resident under a business-as-usual scenario and in the project area. Key parameters are the number of residents in the influence area, mode switch, trip distances and EPs.

The total financing needed (GCF financing plus co-financing) for this project is USD 1,873 million and is expected to be disbursed as follows:

(a) GCF: USD 271.5 million made up of:
- Senior loan: USD 250 million with a 40-year tenor, 10-year grace period and 0.00 percent interest rate; and
- Grant: USD 21.5 million

(b) Co-financing: USD 1,602 million secured as follows:
- Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) - senior loan USD 300 million with a 40-year tenor, 10-year grace period and 6-month London Inter-bank Offered Rate + 2.9 percent;
- Private concessionaire (to be decided through project activity 1.1): equity: USD 250 million; senior loan: USD 750 million.

The private concessionaire invests an estimated USD 1,302 million. The total investment amount stated here has been estimated by the feasibility study carried out during the planning of the project and might be subject to change based on the final design of the project and the engineering design. Based on the assessment realized by the private concessionaire at the tender stage they may require a one-off payment of USD 550 million for the special purpose vehicle (SPV) to cushion the cost of the investment in the public infrastructure required to support the implementation of this project. The USD 550 million will be provided to the Government of Costa Rica as a very concessional loan with a grace period and interest rate to be defined by CABEI. The payment of the USD 550 million from the Government of Costa Rica to the SPV will be disbursed based on the contracted LTV lines of the concessionary. The one-off payment off the government of Costa Rica is subject to change, as it depends on the final investment cost once the project is implemented.

The expected potential outcomes that will be delivered by this project as estimated and presented in the funding proposal and in the answers submitted for this review have been evaluated against the criteria set out in the GCF’s Project Design Document (PDD) as follows:

(a) Replacement of the existing old technology, diesel-fueled, slow, single-track rail system with a fast, comfortable, low-cost, light-carbon electric-powered LRT system.
(b) Not only will this project deliver a modern and efficient urban rail system, with its emissions of CO2h, noxious gases and particulates, but it will also deliver significant climate mitigation benefits from its utilization of low-carbon power from the Costa Rican grid.
(c) The inclusion of NFT facilities (bicycles and cycle lanes) and last-mile connectivity at LRT stations to encourage modal shift towards cycling and walking as well as towards LRT, cycling and light-carbon transport at passenger connection and transfer stations;


---
**Page 92**
---

(e) The entire LRT system will also catalyse modal shifts from other existing transport modes, including public buses, passenger cars (private car, taxi), motorbikes, etc., with passengers who utilize these pre-project modes shifting to the LRT because of its convenience and lower cost compared to their current baseline mode. These modal shifts are the key sources of the GHG emission reductions that will be delivered by this project.

(f) Modal shift will be brought about by the convergence of the LRT and the NMT. The LRT reduces journey times for car and conventional bus users who use 30–60 per cent and will offer an alternative to their current baseline mode, which is private cars. The NMT, as it is planned, will be restructured to promote integration with the LRT and promote sustainable transport choices. Modal shift will also be brought about by the modal shifts that will be estimated using the result of survey of project passengers (what baseline mode would you have used if not for your current use of the LRT in the absence of the LRT) and comparing this to the modal shift model, here there is a 50 per cent chance ensuring that the emission reduction estimated by the modal shift methodology produces conservative estimates.

(g) The independent TAP reviewed the methodologies used in the calculation of the GHG emission reductions expected to be delivered by the interventions of this proposed project. The TAP concluded that the methodologies and assumptions underlying the proposal were judged to be sound and the tools used are expected to deliver reliable and conservative estimates of emissions reductions. The cumulative annual reduction in GHG emissions of 172,246 tCO2e and a lifespan impact of a reduction of 7,622,053 tCO2e.

(h) This independent TAP concludes that this project will deliver a high climate impact potential.


## Paradigm shift potential

### Comprehensive

In the absence of this LRT project, the following baseline conditions will continue to hold in the transport sector of Costa Rica and especially in the catchment area of the GAM:

(i) The Costa Rican transport sector will continue to lead the GHG-emissions growth of the country. It is expected that the total emissions from the transport sector will increase by 189 tCO2e/MIOE in 2035. Without the proposed mitigation interventions of this project, it is expected that the transport sector will continue to grow at a rate of 4 per cent and the transport sector will continue to grow by 64 per cent while under a mitigation scenario they are projected to grow only by 189 tCO2e/MIOE.

(j) The public transport system of Costa Rica (public buses and trains) decreased from a mode share of 64% in 2007 to 54.61 per cent in 2017. During the same period, the use of private cars increased by 38 percentage points from 29.5 per cent to 67.5 per cent.

(k) In the absence of this project, which will deliver a good template for low-carbon transport systems, the continued growth in the number of vehicles and their size will exacerbate GHG emissions and congestion on the roads of the country's metropolis and cities and hence increase the carbon footprint of the country's transport sector.

(l) In the absence of this project, which will deliver a good template for low-carbon transport systems, public funding will not be available and consequently investments in low-carbon transport modes that will utilise the low-carbon electricity available from Costa Rica’s grid will not occur; and


---
**Page 93**
---

### Innovation

Innovation is partially delivered through changes to the status quo that result in value creation (e.g., by introducing a low-carbon transport mode) and ensuring sustainability of the project.


---
**Page 94**
---

process. The Costa Rica LRT investment can deliver such results. The development of the proposed LRT with NMT and last minute access infrastructures in the metropolis is hampered by some of the barriers discussed above and in the funding proposal and some of its annexes. The proposed LRT will be implemented as a public-private partnership (PPP) in the non-carbon transport sector in Costa Rica. The strategy to achieve this was presented convincingly in the feasibility study, which identified the most suitable PPP model for the project. Both the last-minute access infrastructure that are likely to be used by the proposed intervention will not be a good fit with the PPP model and the public sector capacity of the country and perhaps the region; this is important as the development of the LRT systems in Costa Rica, this becomes even more important if the development of the LRT system in Costa Rica is seen as an enabling environment that institutes the implementation of electric vehicles in the country as believed. 1.2.3 Creation of an enabling environment/sustainability of outcomes Among the pieces of the puzzle of this intervention, the search for a private sector investor to finance and operate the last-minute access infrastructure seems to be the issue that still persists some level of risk. The project cannot go forward if a concessionaire is not found. However, the government has already taken steps which will eventually and in a timely manner succeed in selecting a concessionaire for the following reasons: (c) INCOFER, Costa Rica's leading agency for this project (the grantee), has already selected a PPP model that will be used to finance and operate the last-minute access infrastructure on the concessionaire role. According to the information available to the independent TAP, each of these potential concessionaires evaluated as acceptable the CAPEX figures from the feasibility study (and perhaps the front-end costs) presented in preliminary planning. The concessionaires eventually selected will, however, carry out its own detailed engineering and cost estimates for the last-minute access infrastructure that are likely to be more accurate than the earlier estimate. According to information in the funding proposal, changes in the construction cost of the last-minute access infrastructure is expected to increase the bridging fund (USD 550 million) that will be paid to the concessionaire; and (d) A PPP model that will be used in engaging the concessionaire is a very attractive mechanism for financing and operating the last-minute access infrastructure. It can be financed through an SPV established and fully owned by the concessionaire. The government does not participate directly in this financing arrangement, which means that the project sponsor completes construction a fixed sum of USD 550 million and annual operations available to the concessionaire. This is a very attractive mechanism for investors and makes the system less prone to government interference and hence likely to enhance the success of selection of a strong concessionaire. The project team has also been working with the government to ensure that the belief that the selection of concessionaire and hence the successful implementation of an SPV that will play a crucial role in financing and operating the last-minute access infrastructure is not hampered. Therefore, the project plan as presented in the funding proposal and some of the annexes should deliver sustainable outcomes as well as also create an enabling environment that will successfully select a strong concessionaire. 1.2.4 Potential for scaling up and replication Scaling up and replication of this proposed intervention in Costa Rica means the expansion of the project in GCM to the near future and the implementation of the LRT in other major cities in Costa Rica. If the project is successful, as it is expected that it will be a non-carbon transportation project, it will be expected that lessons learned from the success of the LRT will incentivize the introduction of non-train electric vehicles in the country. What are the then?


---
**Page 95**
---

elements and characteristics of the current LRT project implementation that will provide for robustness of the potential for the scaling-up and replication of the intervention? These elements and characteristics can be summarized as follows:

(a) The participation of the private sector in this project, especially the structuring of the financing mechanism, is a positive element. This will make the project less risky and reduce the investment of the government. These are positive impacts that will provide for robustness.

(b) Although the restructuring of the route of the private sector operated public bus system is not part of this project's scope of work, it is understood that this will be done with the regional transport authority (ATR). This will allow for a better integration of the LRT with the NMT as well as the lane-side connectivity interventions, which will provide a model for other cities in Costa Rica. The LRT will also serve as an example to other cities like Costa Rica or in other countries of the region. This is an element and a characteristic that this intervention has that provides for robustness.

(c) A key paradigm shift in the transport sector of Costa Rica is the use of low-carbon electricity as a source of energy for transport. Given that Costa Rica's transport sector is the leading source of GHG emissions in the country, successfully utilizing the low-carbon electricity will provide an opportunity to reduce the carbon footprint and pave the way for replicating the use of electricity as power for other modes (e.g., electric vehicles).

20. Given the three elements discussed above, the independent TAP concludes that the necessary conditions are present within this project to provide a strong potential for the scaling-up and replication of many of the aspects of the current project idea.

### 1.1.5 Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning

The design and implementation plan of the LRT system includes some strategies for knowledge-sharing and learning, which are essential in order to ensure that lessons learned from this project can be shared with other cities. Some are described below to illustrate these strategies for knowledge-sharing and learning built into the LRT project design:

(a) One example is the creation of a grant fund generated from GCF to the creation of knowledge products on lessons learned and the dissemination of the information in such reports. This will be done through the development of outreach activities. Such activities are included in the budget for knowledge-sharing and learning.

(b) Important data and information will be collected in a focused survey covering the entire project area, including the current situation regarding transport modes and behavior. This is operational to determine the transport modes that the passengers would have used in the absence of the LRT. Information so developed will help set the modal shift and behavioral change targets for the project. The results from this type of survey will not only be useful for determining the baseline of this project but also provide valuable information on how to improve the knowledge base regarding the operation of such systems;

(c) The project also includes the collection and generation of standardized data on the impact of soft infrastructure and NMT measures, and the steps to improve the delivery and the impact assessment of such measures. This will be useful to determine the performance of each project component and provide a datum for knowledge sharing and learning.

22. The proposed project will deliver comprehensive and innovative attributes that the independent TAP concludes are essential in order to ensure that this project will deliver an enabling environment that will make the tangible outcomes of the intervention
