1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

### Alignment with the Sustainable Development Goals

The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) that the project intervention is expected to align with are summarized as follows:

- **SDG 3 (good health and well-being):** The fossil fuels (diesel) used as transport fuel in the old diesel rail lines and in the baseline public buses will be curtailed by modal shift leading to a saving in the emissions of greenhouse gases, particulate matter, and other pollutants. This is particularly important in the absence of the LRT. These fossil fuels are sources of emissions of nitrogen gases and particulates. PM2.5s, in particular, matter that would be emitted in the baseline and reduced significantly by the LRT as of particular importance to the health and well-being of the people of Costa Rica.
- **SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy):** The project will deliver on SDG 7 through strengthening the performance of the transport sector, making it more effective in delivering its value addition, which will in turn lead to a reduction in the use of fossil fuels. This is particularly important for the transport industry; in addition, it is expected that infrastructure (rail lines and line roads) will be developed in a way that promotes energy efficiency.
- **SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities):** The baseline public transport situation in the city of San José and the larger GAMA area is characterized by the dominance of private vehicles. This results in high levels of traffic congestion, air pollution (especially diesel), very serious traffic congestion, adding more time to typical travel times; very slow trains on old rail lines, which are not able to compete with other modes of transport such as buses and cars.
- **SDG 13 (climate action):** A primary goal of this project is to alter the GHG emission pathway from that of the historical upward trend that has been forecasted for Costa Rica's transportation sector. The project intervention will lead to significant reductions in emissions, with a significant reduction in the emissions responsible for public health deterioration and climate change. This will have a positive impact on the quality of life for the city dwellers. Hence it can be correctly concluded that the LRT project will deliver on SDG 13.

SDG 13 (climate action): A primary goal of this project is to alter the GHG emission pathway from that of the historical upward trend that has been forecasted for Costa Rica's transportation sector. The project intervention will lead to significant reductions in emissions, with a significant reduction in the emissions responsible for public health deterioration and climate change. This will have a positive impact on the quality of life for the city dwellers. Hence it can be correctly concluded that the LRT project will deliver on SDG 13.

### Economic co-benefits

The success of this first fast electric car will establish a replicable model for other countries as well and facilitate a reduction in the perceived risks of such projects. This project will mobilize into Costa Rica (and beyond) significant financial resources, which will have substantial economic benefits for the country. The enabling environment that will be created by the success of the pioneer project will add more value to this initial economic benefit through the possibility of many other projects being implemented in the future.

25 Other economic co-benefits that have been identified in the project write-up that the independent technical review team has found credible are:

(a) Time savings using the LRT valued at USD 3,354 million (IDOM, 2020).<sup>1</sup>

(b) Operating cost savings estimated as USD 520 million (IDOM, 2020).<sup>2</sup>

(c) Reduced accident rates with cost savings of about USD 53 million (IDOM, 2020).<sup>3</sup>

(d) Reduction in the annual costs associated with health impacts due to emissions of PM2.5, nitrogen oxides and sulfur dioxide estimated at USD 79 million (Grutter Consulting, 2020).<sup>4</sup>

(e) Reduced noise pollution on the basis of the social cost of carbon at USD 550 million (Grutter Consulting, 2020).<sup>5</sup>

(f) Noise emissions valued at USD 6 million (IDOM, 2020).<sup>6</sup>

(g) Overall generation of 1,290 junior jobs during construction and 1,460 direct jobs during operation (IDOM, 2020).<sup>7</sup>

(h) Delivery of 3.3 times more economic benefits than economic cost as an economic internal rate of return at 25 per cent (IDOM, 2020).<sup>8</sup>

26 Generating savings in 2019 and 2019+ will be achieved in the diversion of a sizeable proportion of public funds available in most countries, including Costa Rica. With available public funds being used for other purposes, the economic benefits from this project will reduce the economic losses that will arise from non-implementation of the nationally determined contributions (NDC) targets.

27 In terms of its potential impact at scale, the successful implementation of this project will spare Costa Rica's limited public sector finance, which could then be directed to, for example, healthcare and post-COVID-19 strategies for other needy sectors such as education.


---
**Page 98**
---

### Social and health co-benefits

The project will deliver better air quality in the GAM area and progressively in Costa Rica, where the air quality has been described by the World Health Organization guidelines as moderately polluted. The average annual concentration of PM2.5 is 18 μg/m³, which exceeds the recommended maximum of 10 μg/m³.[1] The LRT, because of its non-combustion technology to power this transport mode and displace some of the fossil fuel used in other modes of transportation, will contribute significantly to these co-benefits. The other project components—the NMT and the last-mile connectivity—will also contribute to the environmental benefits discussed above.

### Environmental co-benefits

The air quality co-benefits of this project result from reduced environmental pollution that will be engendered by the no-combustion powering of the LRT compared with the model that would have been used in the absence of the LRT. The estimated annual PM2.5, NOx and CO emissions associated with the implementation of the proposed LRT are presented in Table 2.

Table 2: Projected reduction of pollutants due to the project (tonnes)

| Parameter | Average Annual Reduction | Cumulative lifetime reduction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PM 2.5 reduction | 7 | 295 |
| NOx reduction | 264 | 11,670 |
| CO reduction | 38 | 56 |

Source: Grutter Consulting (2010) Emission Impact LRT Costa Rica (Annex 19).

These environmental co-benefits, as well as a reduction in noise pollution, will be delivered by the other project components.

### Gender-related benefits

The gender co-benefits of the proposed LRT project in the GAM area were well articulated in the funding proposal and in its Annex B (Gender analysis and action plan). The following three dimensions were included in the gender action plan and built into the project implementation:

(a) Mobility and safety—apart from ensuring that the LRT is fast and easy to carry passengers, special attention has been placed on the special needs of women. This will include: building schemes into the project implementation and operations to facilitate optimal use by women; incorporating measures in the design of the infrastructure that enhance the mobility patterns of women and minimize their exposure to unsafe or insecure transport environments; and ensuring that the infrastructure is gender-sensitive.

(b) Accessibility—ensuring that the new infrastructure, when implemented and operational, takes the requirements of women into consideration. The design, implementation and operation of the LRT and associated infrastructure take into consideration accessibility for women through:

[1] See https://www.istat.it/en/country/costa-rica/04-air-pollution.


---
**Page 99**
---

(0) Acknowledging the flaws in the pre-project system that brought about a barrier for women to economic activities. For example, many of the stations or bus stops, where passengers are picked up are not well lit and secure, especially in the early morning hours when women travel alone at night, posing risks to women and their ability to travel for economic activities;

(0) Ensuring that the new LRT facilities are designed to take into account the needs of female users. This includes ensuring that they have access to clean water, the provision of well-lit female toilets, etc.; 

(0) Monitoring gender issues: The monitoring and project evaluation monitoring programme will include a specific focus on gender-related issues, focusing on results and outcomes at the project level; and

(0) Participating in the gender action plan: A gender action plan is built into the project to ensure gender mainstreaming. Being part of the gender action plan of this project, it will ensure that women are engaged in the transport sector at all stages and decision levels.

The gender action plan will be developed by a gender focal person who will play tangible roles on all levels of activity of this project, including decision-making, working with other stakeholders to ensure that the project is implemented using a gender conflict model at stations and other parts of the integrated system. The project monitoring and evaluation also expect he provides reports on progress in reaching the gender action plan goals.

32 In view of the above-mentioned contributions to various elements of sustainable development, it can be concluded that this project has the potential for significant sustainable development potential and can deliver all the benefits discussed in section 1.3 above. Therefore, independent TAP concludes that the sustainable development potential of this proposed project is high.

### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

#### Needs of the country

It is argued in the funding proposal that, because Costa Rica is considered a primary "hot spot" for climate change in the tropics, this proposed project fits the needs of the country in responding to the various climate change impacts; however, the independent TAP has taken a very different view. The main reasons given by the independent TAP for not including the funding proposal include extreme precipitation, which has increased significantly, being triggered by climate change and is expected to continue increasing in intensity to continue in the future as a result of climate change and to result in a greater frequency or intensity of flooding events. This is one reason why this project was put forward as the key reasons why this project is needed in Costa Rica. To substantiate this claim, the independent TAP has argued that the extreme precipitation for which this project has been designed as a GHG mitigation project, the independent TAP decided to focus on other reasons that can strongly support the argument that this intervention is greatly needed.

The main reasons given by the independent TAP for not including the funding proposal in climate change reasons described in the funding proposal and summarized in para. 33 above) can be summarised as follows:

(0) Given that extreme events in Costa Rica were estimated at about 10-9 MCO2 with the transport sector contributing about 51 per cent of these emissions. The sector is the largest contributor to GHG emissions, and this project will reduce them significantly. This is the proposed project, which will change a major mode of transportation to electric fast rail (the LRT), a logical trigger for a paradigm shift in the transport sector. Therefore,

This will make the project funding attractive to the private entity; provide the concessionaire (in SPV) with the necessary resources to finance its operations during the concession period and, at the end of the concession period, have in place what can become a workable exit strategy for the private sector part of the PPP.

### 1.0 Conclusion

It can be concluded that the financial structuring of the project has been designed in a way that meets the country’s economic and financial needs. The economy of Costa Rica in recent years has been suffering from fiscal weaknesses due to high fiscal deficit. The deficit has lately been the subject of intense debate, with the government having announced that it had reached 60 per cent of gross domestic product in 2020. The negative effect of the COVID-19 pandemic has further exacerbated this situation. Without the implementation and financing of the planned PPF funding model the present project would not have been possible. As argued in the fiscal analysis, the LRT project is a key component of the government’s strategy to address the fiscal challenges prompted by COVID-19 will require higher fiscal spending, which will lead to a deterioration in the fiscal position in 2030. In recognition of this fiscal weakness, the country reached a technical agreement with the World Bank in June 2020 on the implementation of a package of reforms and policies to reduce the fiscal deficit and to promote reforms which allow for lasting and inclusive growth. The LRT project is expected to contribute significantly towards lower borrowing costs for infrastructure planning and implementation of the country’s green recovery strategy, while also contributing to the achievement of the SDGs.

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: High

##### 1.5.1 Evidence of country ownership through participation of relevant stakeholders

Country ownership is strongly related to national stakeholder engagement. The funding proposal provides the following information on the level of stakeholder engagement adopted by the executing agency in the preparation and implementation phases of the project, including the LRT project:

The LRT project has been planned within the context of the Government of Costa Rica’s historical commitment to the international climate change agreements. The country has a long tradition of being at the forefront of combating climate change. During the 1990s, Costa Rica was one of the first countries in Latin America and the Caribbean to sign up for the first joint implementation projects. The country has created within its institutional space the National Green Climate Fund (NGCF) as an instrument to support various


---
**Page 102**
---

strategies that can be said to be precursors of the proposed LRT project. These relevant strategies include:

- (i) Costa Rica's National Climate Change Strategy, which was launched in 2007 and supported by the creation of a Climate Change Department at the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINEA) in 2013;
- (ii) The launching in 2019 of the National Decarbonization Plan 2018–2050, which aims to achieve carbon neutrality with net-zero emissions by 2050 (Government of Costa Rica, 2019); and
- (iii) These strategies and others, such as the National Development and Investment Plan 2017–2030 (NDIC [MINAE], 2015[1]); the National Transport Plan 2011–2035 (MOPP, 2011[2]); the National Energy Plan 2015–2030 (MINAE, 2015[3]); and the National Energy Plan 2015–2030 (MINAE, 2015[4]) and the post-COVID-19 green recovery plan, all have the concept of the LRT as either a part or a core component of these strategies. The national strategies that were considered in the planning and development of the LRT were discussed with relevant stakeholders who hosted workshops on the subject.

Pertinent information presented in the funding proposal and its annexes concerns the responsibility of Costa Rica's transport sector for 52 per cent of the country’s GHG emissions, which is a significant share. The proposal also highlights the need to improve and restructure the transport sector and make it more sustainable as well as reducing the country’s carbon footprint by 30% in 2030 compared with its 1990 baseline. This is playing in the country's carbon footprint, and the fact that the electricity sector is very low-carbon (85%) and has a high potential for further decarbonization to achieve substantial GHG emission reductions. This strategy is articulated in many of the country’s strategic documents, such as the National Climate Change Strategy report and its NDIC. Electric rail as a mitigation measure and specifically the LRT concept are highlighted in many of these strategy documents and formed the foundation for the way the current proposal was developed. The fact that the LRT project is a public-private country of ownership, as the above-mentioned facts must have been considered in the analysis of the environmental impacts of the project. This topic has also been discussed widely with the relevant stakeholders mentioned in paragraph 39(a) above.

Since the submission of the funding proposal and its annexes to the World Bank for the submission, it is very difficult to evaluate the extent of stakeholder interaction in the development of the LRT.

### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

#### 15.3.1 Information on the funding proposal showed that as from the inception of the plan to develop the LRT, especially once the draft of the plan was available, the Government of Costa Rica recognized the importance of engaging with civil society organizations (CSOs) and other relevant stakeholders.

In December 2018, as an example of the result of many interactions with stakeholders, the mayors of the 15 affected municipalities signed a framework agreement for intermunicipal cooperation in the development of the LRT. This agreement was intended to support the development and the development of urban equipment and infrastructure complementary to the LRT. The framework agreement also included an annex that defined the criteria for the selection of projects, which were discussed in multiple round tables with all stakeholders.

It is not clear from the funding proposal how the project team ensured that relevant stakeholders (stakeholder *c*) engagement continued during the planning and design of the LRT. This is a critical issue because the success of the project depends on the successful implementation of the project. Information in the funding proposal, however, showed that for the construction period (the engineering design stage), continuous involvement of relevant stakeholders must be engendered.

INOFER, according to the information available, must design and deliver to CABEI a clear plan for stakeholder engagement during the planning phase, including the activities carried out, complete with evidence. During the operation of the project, INOFER is also expected to provide evidence that it has engaged in regular communication (at least biannual monitoring reports by INOFER or the concessionaire to CABEI).

#### 15.3.2 As part of the project plan, given that better and faster alternatives will be provided to users of public transport services, the LRT will not compete with private sector operators of those bus routes but will be considered as soon as the LRT is operational.

In response to this statement, the AE informed CABEI that the LRT will not compete with these private operators, whose livelihood would be terminated. The AE informed the independent TAP that the LRT will not compete with private operators and that no negotiations were held with those private operators while this project was on the drawing board and they were made aware of the fact that the LRT is a public transport system and not an alternative route because that mode is more comfortable, faster and will avoid traffic congestion on the roads within GAM. The independent TAP understands that they are willing to go along with the most current information provided by the AE.

The AE also informed CABEI that the LRT will not compete with private operators of their bus routes restricting activities. These stakeholders are also aware that public bus route restrictions will be applied in GAM and that the LRT is a public transport system, none of whose being offered alternative routes is outside the control and scope of INOFER.

#### 15.3.3 The independent TAP is of the opinion that stakeholder engagement of this project is robust but could be improved by ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are informed about their roles in the development of this kind of transport infrastructure.


### Executing entity of the executing entity

#### 15.4 As stated in the funding proposal, the executing entity for this project (INOFER) and the Government of Costa Rica have signed a loan agreement with CABEI for the purpose of financing the GCF process. INOFER is an independent statutory corporation established to operate and maintain public transport services using its own resources and its own assets. Within INOFER, an entity has been put in place to support and assist the institution in all activities related to the concession, the monitoring of the feasibility studies, the bidding


---
**Page 104**
---

stage of the concession, compliance with conditions precedent, the design stage and the construction stage. INCOFER will submit to CAREB quarterly reports on the physical execution of the project, compliance with the environmental management plans and in general the compliance with the terms and conditions of the concession contract. CAREB will realise biannual supervision missions and will also contract an external supervisory entity. INCOFER will be responsible for the preparation of a technical and financial feasibility study of the LRT as part of preparing this funding proposal and delivered the same for final approval by the concession company, one of which will be selected to implement the project.

* A capacity assessment of INCOFER was conducted by CAREB. The assessment concluded that the institution has the necessary human resources and technical expertise to manage the concession.
The institution carries out internal audits periodically and is subject to audits by the Auditor General and the Ministry of Planning and Economic Policy. In addition, an independent external auditor has been appointed for the concession period. INCOFER complies with all national laws regarding procurement and no inconsistencies over the past 10 years have been identified.
* The Government of Costa Rica is the beneficiary of the grant for component 2 and the concessionaire receives a one-off payment of US$50 million (3 tranches) – from the concessionaire to the government. This payment will be made in three instalments: USD 80 million (1st tranche), USD 90 million (2nd tranche) and USD 80 million (3rd tranche). The Government of Costa Rica will receive a total of USD 250 million against committed LRT construction and lines ready for operations. This one-off payment is not a loan commitment in the SPC but a prepayment of part of the concession fee.
The GCC contribution to the project will be fully paid by the Government of Costa Rica and reduce its financial burden. In addition, it has been estimated that the Government of Costa Rica will save approximately USD 10 million per annum for payment of services to the concessionary (apart from the USD 50 million as part of the grant) over a period of 20 years.
The concession fee is included in the government’s annual budget. As such, this prepayment remains a hanging risk that may negatively affect project success each operating year.

### The capability of the accredited entity

* The AIF for this project will be CAREB, a supranational development bank focused on Central America, which was founded in 1960. The bank’s objective is to promote the economic integration and sustainable development of the countries that make up the region.
Costa Rica is one of the founder members of CAREB. During the period 2010–2018 CAREB approved USD 35 billion for projects in Central America, including Costa Rica. CAREB holds the best credit rating in Latin America.* It has a total authorised capital of USD 7 billion.
The concession contract is subject to the approval of the Government of Costa Rica. CAREB requires that its operations comply with environmental and social standards, based on international best practices, aligned with its Environmental and Social Policy. In the transport


---
**Page 105**

sector, CABEI has realized various PPPs in Costa Rica, including Ruta 27, Windfarm Valle Central, Altos de Magdalena. CABEI will also have two supervising entities for the loan, one contractor and one consultant. The project is being structured as a public-private partnership with an international lender, in order to have sufficient external technical know-how during the entire process of project implementation.

Based on the analysis presented in sections 1.5.1-1.5.4 above, the independent TAP considers that the country ownership of this LRT project submission can be ranked as high.


### The Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

#### 1.6 Financial structure

The proposed project is requesting grant and debt financing from GCF and other co-financing sources from the amount of USD 1,873.3 million. The GCF contribution amounts to USD 271.3 million (of which USD 250 million will be sourced as concessive equity), while the remaining USD 250 million. The complementary co-financing will come from the following sources: USD 300 million (of which USD 250 million will be sourced as concessive equity) and USD 1,052 million (with terms to be agreed with the lender). The details of the GCF contribution and the funds were discussed in an earlier section of this report (see table 1 and para. II above).

### Co-financing, leveraging and mobilizing long-term investment

#### 1.2.2.

The USD 273.1 million requested from GCF will be complemented by USD 1,602 million from CABEI and the selected concessionaire. The senior loan from CABEI as well as the sum from CABEI and the concessionaire will be used to finance the project’s CAPEX (capital expenditure) and O&M costs. This will yield an indicative leverage ratio between public investments (CABEI and GCF funding) and private investments of about 1:2.4. This can be considered an effective and efficient use of funds for this project.

#### 5. Other key performance indices of the financing scheme of this project can be summarized as follows:

(a) The majority, specifically 60 per cent co-financing funds will be co-financed basically through private sector capital (around 70 per cent) and through a loan from CABEI (around 16 per cent).

(b) The GCF loan has a lower interest rate than the CABEI loan, which is important for the Government of Costa Rica as it reduces fiscal spending.

(c) GCF financing ensures the project viability and assists in the crowding-in of private capital into the project.

(d) The concessionaire will receive a subsidy of slightly less than 60 per cent through funds provided by the GCF loan. This is to be used for the construction phase, as well as a CAPEX bridging fund (neither equity nor debt) to make the project financially feasible for the concessionaire.

(e) Using the results of the LRT, a financial analysis showed that the project can deliver an economic internal rate of return after about 26 years.10

(f) The independent TAP is of the opinion that these financial performances discussed in paragraph (e) are consistent with the expected long-term performance of the project when compared with benchmarks of similar projects recently funded by GCF.

#### 1.3. Cost-efficiency

The project is expected to deliver about 7.62 MgCO2eq of GHG emission reductions over the lifetime of the project. The efficiency metrics that will be delivered by this project interventive measure are as follows:

(a) Total project financing = USD 1,873.3 million;

(b) Requested GCF amount = USD 273.1 million;

(c) Expected lifetime GHG emissions = 76,224,805 tCO2eq;

(d) Estimated cost per ton (tCO2eq) = USD 249/tCO2eq; and

(e) The economic benefits are the justification for realizing subsidies as without these the LRT would not be established.


---
**Page 107**
---

Estimated GCF cost per tCO2eq (Bd)/t(CO2eq) = USD 36/CO2eq/t

The independent TAP concludes that the GCF mitigation cost of USD 36/CO2eq/t as presented above, when compared with other mitigation projects funded by GCF is high. However, when the external benefits of the project due to time savings, positive health and environmental impacts, and the potential for additional co-benefits are added to the project's benefits, this may represent excellent value for money. The possibility that a concessionary loan from the World Bank or other financial institution to support this project has been given by the Board remains a tangible risk to the cost-effectiveness of this project. However, it is noted that there have not yet been any discussions with potential private sector developers who have registered their interest in this project; the independent TAP is of the opinion that this remaining risk may turn out to have only a marginal impact on the success of the FPP investment.

The independent TAP therefore concludes that the efficiency and effectiveness of implementation of this proposed project will be moderately high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve this LRT project proposed for implementation in the GAM area of San Jose, Costa Rica.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

Thanks for the review.
Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the current global energy-related GHG emissions and is greater than any other energy end-use sector. GHG emissions from transport are anticipated to rise from today's levels by nearly 20% by 2030 and close to 50% by year 2050 unless drastic action is taken. The recently released World Urban Transport Report of the United Nations Economic Commission for Europe (UNECE) requires changing this transport emissions trajectory and GCF interventions in the transport sector. The project goes beyond establishing solely an urban mass transit system but builds a comprehensive strategy to reduce GHG emissions from all modes of urban transportation and with components of transit oriented development. Long-term structural changes are thus required towards ultra-low carbon emission mobility in the larger urban zone of the metropolitan area.

1. Introduction

Costa Rica has made great advances on gender issues over the last few decades. This has responded to some extent to the granting of rights and other social and economic advances in the country. The country has registered a sustained increase in per capita income and human development levels, which allows for better access to services, opportunities and a higher level of quality of life.

For example, from 1990 to 2019, the country has increased the per capita income of its inhabitants almost 7 times, as shown in Figure 1 (World Bank, 2020). Also, the Human Development Index (HDI) (United Nations Development Programme [UNDP], 2020) presents an upward trend since 1996, where the country went from having a medium to high level of development, surpassing the average level at the global level as shown in Figure 2.

GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN


---
**Page 114**
---

In addition to social progress, Costa Rica enjoys political stability, given the uninterrupted nature of its democracy since 1948, the longest in Latin America. In the last 15 years, economic growth has averaged 4%, a trend that continues at 3.8% according to the World Economic Forum (WEF) and the Economist Intelligence Unit (EIU). The country has changed its trade matrix from being an agro-exporter country to diversifying its production and offering skilled labor (OECD, 2017). This has led to an improvement in the quality of jobs in the country, where both men and women have benefited.

Poverty is a female issue. Although the country has good economic and social performance, poverty levels are higher among women than men, especially for children under five years old, single mothers, and the elderly. According to the OECD (2017), there is an increase in income inequality because skilled worker wages and contributory pensions grew more.

Costa Rica has focused on the institutional and regulatory framework in place to improve the conditions of women's work. The country has made progress in social and economic levels; however, gender gaps are persistent. According to data from the World Economic Forum (WEF) (FEIM, 2019), at the last issue of the Global Gender Gap Report, Costa Rica was ranked 13th out of 153 countries. The country occupies the second position in Latin America and the Caribbean region after Chile.

In addition to gender gaps in access to education, there are great differences between the training and skills women acquire when compared with men. Finally, a better gender equality situation at the national territory responds mainly to improvements in access to economic opportunities for women who work in the informal sector or who do not participate in the paid labor force.

Although Costa Rican women are working at a higher rate than almost two decades ago, there are still barriers to access when compared to men. Only about half of the women who are able to work are included in the active labor force (according to recent data from the National Institute of Statistics and Census (INEC), 2020). The net labor participation rate of women at the end of 2019 was 54.1, and this situation worsened with the arrival of the COVID-19 health emergency. By the second half of 2020, 23.1% of women were unemployed (a figure that is 6 percentage points higher than in 2018) versus 17.4% for men (a figure that is 10 percentage points higher (13.8%). The above situation denotes an exacerbation of vulnerability on the part of women regarding the quality, stability and productivity of their jobs.

For the pre-COVID-19 census, a research study by the State of the Nation Program (2019) concludes that Costa Rican women are more likely to work in low-productivity sectors. Sixty-seven percent of women who work do so in low-productivity sectors. In addition, the 2015 Latinobarómetro report (Latinobarómetro, 2015) shows that gender norms about women's roles in the workplace are far from egalitarian. In Costa Rica, 47% of the population believes that women should work outside the home only if their partner does not earn enough. A much higher percentage when compared to countries such as Chile (20%) or Uruguay (27%).


---
**Page 115**
---

All of the above means that the closing of the educational gaps has not translated into the productive and economic participation of women. According to data from the Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) [ECLAC, 2013] in Costa Rica, 73% of women do not have an income or income three times more than men (10.5%), a percentage slightly higher than the rest of women in the region (29%). Not having their own income restricts women's autonomy, and therefore limits their decision-making and results in a deterioration or slowdown in the human development indices. Women’s economic participation is also limited by the fact that they are concentrated in low-paying jobs with unstable hours, which in turn play a key role in the development of countries, contributing significantly to the reduction of poverty and inequality and to the increase of the GDP.

The following report will detail all main challenges facing women in Costa Rica. It is mainly an analysis of gender equality in access to education, labor market, access to opportunities and services, physical autonomy and decision-making. This diagnosis will make it possible to inform adjacent and direct activities of the investment in the electric power train in order to increase gender equality, a necessary condition for achieving higher levels of development in the country.

The following analysis is based on:

- Desk review of different sources of information available online. These sources range from national statistics, data and reports (such as INEC, UCR, INAMU, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Culture, Ministry of Public Works and Transport, Ministry of Science and Technology, Ministry of Justice, General Comptroller of the Republic, State of the Nation, among others)and international organizations such as the United Nations Development Program (UNDP), Inter-American Development Bank, World Economic Forum, World Bank, United Nations Organization, GIZ, AFD, Latobarometer, etc.)
- Consultation with stakeholders: to ensure synergies and alignment with initiatives that are being designed or implemented in the current area of the project. The following are the organization that were consulted:
    * GET/UNDP
    * INAMU
    * Multilevel Technical Table (Office of the First Lady, the National Institute of Housing and Urban Development (INHU), the Ministry of Public Works and Transport (MOPT) and the Inter-American Advisory Services (IFAM) and the National Union of Local Governments (UNGL), the Ministry of Planning, the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE), the Ministry of Finance, the National Council for Women (CNEM), the National Commission on Gender Equality (CNEG), the Ministry of Public Works and Transport (MOPT). On the private sector side, the Chamber of Commerce, the Chamber of Construction and the Council for Real Estate Development (CODI) participate, as well as international cooperation actors such as the United Nations Development Program (UNDP), Inter-American Development Bank, World Bank, United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), etc.

1 World Bank studies [2013] indicate that in the first decade of the 21st century female labor market participation has increased from extreme poverty by 30% and inequality by 28% on the region. On the other hand, Cobas y Teigeler (2016) conclude that if female labor participation in Latin America and the Caribbean were to reach the level of male labor participation, per capita GDP would be 16% higher.

# Background

Costa Rica has been committed to closing gender gaps for almost half a century. For more than 45 years, the Costa Rican government has generated institutional actions that promote gender equality.

For example: (i) In 1974 the Office of Programs for Women and the Family was created within the Ministry of Culture; (ii) in 1980 the National Commission on Equality between Men and Women was created; (iii) in 1986 the Commission of Real Equality for Women, (iv) in 1998 the National Women's Institute (INAMU) was created as a decentralized institution led by the Minister for the Status of Women.

Costa Rica has a consolidated regulatory framework to empower women and close gender gaps. One of the most important instruments is the implementation of the National Policy for Effective Equality between Women and Men (PIEG) and its 2018-2030 action plan, as a follow-up to the PIEG 2007-2017.

The implementation of the PIEG seeks to generate inter-institutional coordination at both the public and private levels, focusing on areas such as employment, health and education. The PIEG focuses on four strategic areas: (i) access to productive resources; (ii) access to decision-making positions; (iii) power distribution; and it also seeks to expand access to opportunities for women in multiple sectors, including public transportation and climate change.

INAMU acts as the coordinator of the PIEG and due to its role as the Ministry of Women, it is responsible to engage with other institutions that work towards gender equality. The PIEG 2018-2030 established 4 strategic areas to ensure efficiency during the implementation of the Policy:

* **Coordination:** INAMU is the responsible of the coordination of the PIEG.
* **Policy Implementation:** The Ministry of Women, in collaboration with other ministries of the Executive Power (Ministry of Public Education, Ministry of Labor, Ministry of Health and Rectory of the Social Sector, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of National Planning), Legislative Assembly, the Judicial Power, the Supreme Electoral Tribunal and the Municipalities. These institutions need to approve the PIEG 2018-2030 action plan in order for it to be put into practice.
* **Main Function:** Its main function is to provide follow-up, support and political incidence for the advancement in compliance with the results of the PIEG and its action plans.
* **Technical Support:** It has a technical structure, and it has representatives from institutions and sectors that are linked to the implementation of the PIEG. INAMU and the representatives hold periodic meetings every 2 months.

* **Local Level:** Local governments throughout the Development Regional Councils will be in responsible to implement the PIEG.


---
**Page 117**
---

At the local level, the INAMU coordinates with the Municipal Governments to ensure the application of the PEG, named the Oficina Intersectorial de la Mujer (Women's Management Office), OFIM. In 1996, the National Government created the OFIMs. Across the entire territory, there are 67 OFIMs out of a total of 81 municipalities that have the municipal structure. They carry out actions in different thematic areas, such as attention to domestic violence, promotion of women's rights, prevention of economic rights, civic participation and political rights, health, and education. They carry out actions to promote gender equality from an integrated approach across the municipal work. The authorities develop gender equality policies and incorporate the gender perspective into the municipality’s budgetary structure. INAMU provides technical assistance for the OFIMs in their work. Some have established municipal policies as a strategy for gender mainstreaming (INAMU, 2021). OFIMs have limited resources to carry out this work focusing on addressing cases of domestic violence.

Despite these efforts, women are still underrepresented within policy and decision making. The National Government is dominated by the political party within the cabinet, (Rivera, 2018) however, that does not translate to other policy making structures. For instance, a third of the total decision-making position with the national government, public institutions management and the Parliament are held by women (as opposed to the 66 percent held by men), (FEM, 2019). There is not recent data on how the public institutions are managed in Costa Rica. Women hold only a small percentage of positions within the private sector and underrepresented: only 15 percent of top management positions belong to women, while 85 percent belong to men. (FEM, 2019)

Also, data from the ECCLAS (ECCLAS, 2020) indicates that only a third of the judges are women (33.3%). In 2018, there were 47 female judges out of 145 women (14.8% in 2016 and less than half of city councils (45.6%) are represented by women. This represents a constraint for women to be involved in decision making at all levels, including decisions at a local level (e.g., women are underrepresented as mayors) where a lot of program services are designed and implemented. Without a full representation, women's needs are not being able to be addressed as they are unable to fully enjoy access to opportunities and participate as agents of change.

Less traditional sectors for women, such as transport, still have significant gender gaps. In Costa Rica, nearly 60% of public transport users are women, yet there are very few women in decision-making positions within the sector. Women make up only a small percentage of bus drivers and conductors. This figure is lower when it comes to cab drivers, where women represent less than 10% of the drivers in this service*. In the case of the passenger train in the Metropolitan Area, there is only one woman on the staff who represents 25% of the total number of employees. The lack of female representation can hinder gender equality and address persistent gaps in the transportation sector. It is important that projects that support initiatives such as the Greater Metropolitan Area LRT include actions that include the gender perspective and propose indicators that measure concrete results. To achieve this, it is necessary that these operations have a gender perspective from their design (at the level of diagnostics), proposed


---
**Page 118**
---

activities and measurement of results)), in order to improve the inclusion of women in all spheres: decision-making, service providers and users of the proposed services, and that their implementation be through an action plan.

There are sensitive gaps in the production of information that make it impossible to quantify more precisely the gaps and challenges women face. There are mainly deficiencies in recognizing the geographic characteristics of the population and there is only the possibility of using the 2011 Census (INEC, 2011) to understand the situation of women in Costa Rica. The lack of valid information, for example, is a whole lack of statistics related to physical, psychological and sexual violence that impacts the lives of women in both public and private spaces. In the absence of sources of information that systematically collect data and gaps, it is not possible to implement solutions that address specific problems, or to deal with them in the most efficient manner with tailored solutions that prevent or address their incidence.

### 3. Economic Participation of Women in Costa Rica

32.7% of women in Costa Rica have no income of their own*, compared to 10.5% of men. Women also face greater complications in rural areas, where about 42.8% (11.3% of men) of women have no income, a higher number than in urban areas (29.4%; and only 10.2% of men) [Figure 1](CEPALSTAT, 2020). Although the gender gap is wide, the country has achieved a progressive reduction since the beginning of 2000, where nearly half of the women did not have their own income.

Without income, women are at a disadvantage due to the loss of their autonomy over access goods and services, limiting their ability to make decisions that affect their lives. According to data from CEPESTAT (CEPESTAT, 2020) in Costa Rica, 47% of women do not have their own income, five times more than men in couples (Figure 2). This also responds to cultural patterns and gender norms revealed by the Latinobarometer (2015): 43% of the Costa Rican population considers that women should only work if their partner's salary is not sufficient.

The proportion of women without income in the country is slightly higher than the average recorded for the region. For example, according to data from 2014, for every 100 men aged 20 to 59 living in poor households, there are only 37 women with no income (INEC, 2020). The situation is similar when considering poverty rates: Costa Rica has a higher poverty rate than Latin America (18.2%) and for countries such as Honduras (10.7%), Guatemala (16.9%), El Salvador (15.3%) and Nicaragua (14.2%); although it is exceeded by the Dominican Republic (13.2%), Chile (13.6%) and Uruguay (14.2%) (Benavente et al., M.C., 2014).

Poverty tends to be more prevalent among women than men, according to data for 2019 (INEC, 2020), poverty is present in almost a quarter of the population, and about one out of every five people lives in extreme poverty. The area with the highest proportion of poor households is the Branga Region, where 3 out of every 5 households is in poverty and 3.2% live in extreme poverty. In the case of the Central Region, 17% of households are in poverty and 4.8% live in extreme poverty. The trend for both regions is a slight increase in the number of households led by women, from 39.0% to 41.5%. By 2019, 48% of all poor households were headed by men, and 49.38% of all households in extreme poverty. In other words, there is a slight increase in the number of households led by women.

In terms of urbanization, it can be observed that more than half of poor households are women heads of household in urban areas (44.42% of total households) than in rural areas (32.02%), yet more than half of poor households in urban areas are headed by women (51%) and 54% of households in extreme poverty. In the case of the Central Region, the pattern is repeated: more than half of poor households are headed by women (56%). The same trend can be observed for the Branga Region.

There are territorial differences associated with the multidimensionality of poverty and female head of household. For some of the cantons in the area affected by the Passenger Train (identified with * in Figure GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN

By superimposing these data on household headship with other data on inequality (Gini index, see Figure 3), or even poverty (poverty gap or severity), it can be observed that there is a strong correlation between the percentage of households headed by women and a lower Gini Index. This is a simple explanation that describes that the higher the employment rate, the higher the hypothetical employment rate, which would lead to a decrease in poverty and an increase in household income, thus reducing basic deficiencies. As an approximation, this exercise makes it possible to analyze how different types of indicators vary with respect to the effect on the train; however, it should be noted that the difference may not necessarily be due to the neighboring railway infrastructure, but rather to the proximity of the city center.

On the other hand, there are cultural patterns and gender norms that extend into time-use behaviors and the sexual division of labor. The use of time in unpaid work is one of the main barriers for women to enter the labor market under equal conditions and obtain their own income. According to data from the National Survey on the Use of Time (2017) [INEC, 2017] on average, women in Costa Rica spend more than twice as much time as men on unpaid domestic work ([33 hours and 38 minutes per week vs. 13 hours and 26 minutes per week, respectively]). The tasks that demand the greatest investment of women's time are:

- **Household chores:** This includes cooking, cleaning, washing clothes, and other similar activities.
- **Childcare:** This involves taking care of children in their daily activities, in addition to investigating topics such as unpaid domestic work, free time, personal needs, among others.

GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN


---
**Page 123**
---

are related to food and care. Only about of children between the ages of 3 and 5 in Costa Rica attend daycare centers or pre-school education, while in OECD countries the average is 84% (OECD, 2017). In addition, women are kept encumbered for the whole age group, which means that this type of work up to three times more than men (39 hours compared to 13 hours and 40 minutes). Also, the age group where they spend the most hours in unpaid work is women between 36 and 54 (about 41 hours a week).

Women, then, have less time to get and keep a job. The net participation rate of women in 2020 is 44% (6 percentage points lower than that registered during the period of 2019), with a decrease of 7.1% (a 15% percentage point) compared to the same period in 2018. During the period of COVID-19, this situation aggravated unemployment for men, but especially for women. While the unemployment rate for women was around 15% during 2019, for the same period in 2020, women's unemployment reached 30%, both figures being higher than those registered by men.

In working age, there are more women out of the workforce than men. In the country, the number of women of working age (1,988,513) is very similar to the number of men (2,001,138). However, the number of unemployed women (467,000) is significantly lower than that of men (585,000). On the other hand, the number of unemployed men (281,939) is only slightly higher than the number of women (269,434). This means that in relative terms, although the size of the male and female populations are similar, women have a greater chance of facing barriers to access the labor market (wingshing, ability, etc.) and also of being unemployed.

Women have less access to social security than men. Of the total number of women in the workforce, 27.9% do not have social security, a percentage slightly higher than the 25.2% registered for men. During the COVID-19 emergency, nearly 46% of women ceased to have access to the right to social security, A proportion that is more than twice the figure for men who did so (about equal access to social security and to conditions of work). Women are therefore more likely to be in a situation of greater proportion than men.

During the COVID-19 emergency, women with lower levels of education have been the most affected by unemployment. According to INEC data (INEC, 2020) when looking at the educational levels of those who lost their jobs during the global pandemic, 54% of women with incomplete primary or lower levels of education lost their jobs compared to the previous period, a much higher figure than 24% for men. For their part, 10.3% of women with university degrees were also affected by unemployment, but men in the same situation only had an unemployment rate of 5%.

In addition to the economic slowdown and the increase in unemployment as a result of the emergence of COVID-19, there are other reasons why women are unable to access the labor market. For example, women report that they have to take on a greater proportion of household tasks than men.

Access to opportunities to reach the most productive jobs is limited for women due to gender patterns that are reinforced by social norms and stereotypes (OECD, 2015) as a result, the gender education gap is closed in the country. In general terms, almost 100% percent of the population is literate (97.9 percent of women and 97.8 percent of men), and for the primary, secondary and tertiary education cycles, women attend with the same incidence or more than men (56.3 vs. 56.4; 64.9 vs. 60.1; and 60.7


---
**Page 124**
---

percent vs. 50 percent). However, when an analysis is made of access to different fields and, in particular, those careers or professions that are more productive, women have less opportunities than men.

When women pursue professional careers, they focus on lower productivity sectors or fields of care. According to the data (FEM, 2019), only 3% of women who enter university education do so primarily in the FEM, 2019). The gap between men and women in more productive fields such as those associated with science, technology, mathematics and engineering has reduced spaces for women: only 4% of women decide to study for this type of career (Figure 7).

There are barriers to entry to the STEM sectors from early stages of the life cycle and this is reflected in the jobs that are occupied by women. In Costa Rica, only 44% of science and technology researchers are women, a lower proportion than in countries such as Trinidad and Tobago (55%), Argentina (51%) and Uruguay (60%) (Science and Technology Indicators Network, 2017). Data from the National Learning Institute show that women occupy little more than half of the positions in computer science research (7% of students are women), telecommunication and telematics (20%), electricity and electronics (6%). In addition, women make up 32% of the total number of students enrolled in engineering careers and 36% of the graduates.

The lower quality of women's jobs, coupled with problems of discrimination, makes it difficult to close the gender wage gap. In relation to labor income, women earn on average about 90% of what men earn (INEC, 2020). There are important differences in the types of jobs men and women hold. For example, the income received by women is lower than that earned by men in all professional categories except for managers, in both public and private sector jobs; women earn less than men (7% less and 15% less, respectively). Women in lower-skilled positions receive an average of 15% less than men in the same position. It is important to note that, in addition, salaried women earn an average of 4% more than men. (Chart 8)

Women's ventures tend to be less productive and less profitable than those of men. Women entrepreneurs are mainly concentrated in micro and small enterprises in commerce, services, and manufacturing (there is little representation in high-productivity sectors or STEM), and have lower profitability rates compared to male entrepreneurs.

According to the World Bank Enterprise Survey (World Bank, 2020b) for Costa Rica in 2010, the percentage of firms with female owners is 43.5%, which is very similar to the regional average (43%). However, only 15.4% of firms have women in senior management positions, a lower proportion than that recorded for peers in the region (21.8%).

In Costa Rica, this translates into the fact that women are concentrated in sectors where they work directly or indirectly, and presents barriers at all levels for them. According to the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) (J08, 2016) in the country, less than one-fifth (17%) of the human resources employed in the

Female drivers have been less aware almost twice as often as men. This gap is greater when analyzed by the type of license drivers have for productive work. Only 10% of licensed bus drivers are women, and between 7% and 12% of airport and regular cab concessions are owned by women (although this does not mean that they are the drivers of the vehicle). Women also hold a very small percentage of positions in the public administration sector, with only 3.5%, which is less than half what men have. Women occupy a technical position within INCOFDER.

Other sources of information denote the same problems and gaps in women's access to the labor market. According to Valeria Lanza (Lentini; Valenza, 2020) as a result, vertical segregation is present in all sectoral planning areas such as education, transportation, logistics and communications networks are predominantly represented by men, while third-party care sectors such as education, health and other social services are dominated by women. This situation is further exacerbated when the consequences of the health crisis caused by COVID-19 are considered, since 35% of Costa Rican jobs will have high negative impacts on their jobs (ILO, 2020), but particularly for women, as they represent more women are more prominent. ### Advances in Women’s Sexual and Reproductive Health

Costa Rica’s historical investments in the health sector have succeeded in offering its women a better quality of life thanks to the contributions made in the area of sexual and reproductive health. In particular, the country has reduced the maternal mortality rate by 90% since 1985, while the incidence of adolescent pregnancy and births to underaged parents is decreasing. This is due to a multidisciplinary social security system that is also coordinated with other relevant actors in health, education, and social assistance.

The fertility rate has been systematically reduced for decades. While in 1960 women averaged 6.7 children per woman, this number dropped to 3.58 children per woman by 1990. In 2010, women had an average of 2.58 children, and by 2015 the fertility rate was 1.88. The current incidence is estimated at a figure of 1.77 children (World Bank, 2020).

The maternal mortality rate is one of the lowest in the region; yet inequalities persist within the country. While the regional maternal mortality rate in Latin America and the Caribbean is estimated at 76 deaths per 100,000 births, in the country this figure is estimated at 27 maternal deaths per 100,000 births. This figure has been on a downward trend since 2000, when there were 40 deaths for every 100,000 births. The provinces of Limón and Alajuela have a higher proportion of deaths of this type than other provinces (INEC, 2020).

Costa Rica has committed to a comprehensive agenda in order to contribute to the reduction of the number of adolescents who become mothers and has managed to reduce its incidence in recent years. According to data from the INEC (INEC, 2005-2010; 2018), 18.7% of births in the country were to women under 19, but for the provinces of Limón and Alajuela this figure was 34%. In the provinces of Puntarenas and Limón, the data is higher than the national average (15% for each, respectively).

The presence of a pregnancy during adolescence and teenage motherhood are associated with the initiation of sexual relations. Despite the existence of a law that prevents this, inappropriate relationships (adolescent sexual relations) continue to be common in Costa Rica. According to data from the National Survey of Sexual and Reproductive Health (ENSIS) of the Ministry of Health and Central American Population (Ministry of Health and COP, 2016) for the year 2015 (NSIS-15), the average age of sexual debut for women over 15 is 17, but for men it was 18.

Women aged between 15 and 19 years old who were between the ages of 10 and 13 and reported that their partner was at least 10 years older than them by 59.6%, and men older than 20 reported that their partner was 5 to 10 years younger than them by 8.5%. This means that in many of these cases these girls and adolescents had sexual relations with older men, a fact that constitutes a crime in Costa Rica*. Additionally, according to data from the Population Census and Housing Census of 2011 (INEC, INECE, 2011) this is the case of girls.

* The Costa Rican Criminal Code provides an express prohibition on sexual relations with persons under the age of 15, even when they are in a relationship. It also prohibits marriage and considers sexual relations with girls under the age of 13 to be rape (Articles 156, 157 and 159 of the Criminal Code).

GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN


---
**Page 128**
---

8.6% of girls and adolescents between 12 and 19 years of age and 2.6% of their male peers have ever lived in a marital union of some kind, either positive or negative. Impact on children is evident: 74.5% of boys and additional 30% declare that they are not or have never been in a union do not attend the educational system, compared to those who are not or have never been in a union (17%). In addition, among those who are or have been in a union, 88.3% declare that they do not work, and of these 57.3% only do unpaid domestic work (United Nations Population Fund [UNPF], 2018).

A key element in assessing the impact of women in Costa Rica to decide about their own bodies is the state of contraception. The three main methods are female sterilization, male condom, and oral contraceptives. The three methods have different levels of effectiveness. The use of contraceptive methods varies according to age group, educational level and geographical area. The vast majority of unmarried adolescents do not use any contraceptive method (only 24.6% report using some method). 76.2% of unmarried women who do not use in the central region they use the male condom method and only 59.3% of unmarried women whose educational level is up to secondary school.

### 5. Violence Against Women in Costa Rica

The specific data on violence against women is outdated and has not been followed up. The last specific survey on this topic - the National Survey on Violence against Women (ENICM) - is from 2004*. Information has been collected in other instances, however, this impedes temporal and regional comparisons. *The ENICM was conducted by INEC, with technical assistance of different levels such as INAMU, INEC and the Research Center for Women's Studies of the University of Costa Rica have become involved in promoting the agenda for generating data and information on this issue.

The 2004 specialized survey on violence against women shows that 57.7% of the women surveyed reported having been the victim at least one incident of physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives, while 38.2% declared to have suffered a situation of violence at time same. Incidence among women between the ages of 25 and 49, this percentage was higher (62%), although the situation during women's childhood and early adolescence is not much different: 48% of the women surveyed reported having suffered some form of violence in their childhood.

The couple and the family are the areas where women have historically been most exposed to violence. 45.5% of women report having experienced physical violence in these areas, 38.2% sexual violence and 49.6% psychological violence from their partner (ENICM 2004). According to ENICS 15, in public spaces, close to 80% of the women surveyed stated that they have suffered a situation of violence at some time.

* ENICM collects information on physical and sexual violence and harassment.


GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN


---
**Page 129**
---

In the student or work environment, close to 70% of the female population reports having suffered some type of sexual violence (almost always or always), in the case of men, the percentage is 60%.1

The public transport sector also concentrates high levels of violence. The presence of acts of violence against women in this context is not limited to sexual harassment but includes other types produced by the use of buses, trains or cabs. According to data from INAMU (2019), Costa Rican women constantly experience sexual harassment, sexual violence and other types of violence while they are users. The perpetrators are usually men and use different means to commit these acts, such as verbal abuse in public spaces; in domestic losses for family members or not being able to safely access the jobs or other services outside the home. There are limitations with respect to the generation of data and statistics that show the magnitude of the problem of violence against women in the facilities and services offered by INCOFER. There is evidence that sexual harassment occurs on public transport, but there is no information about the incidence of acts of violence or sexual harassment against women for different modes of public transportation.

In other areas, there is a lack of and limited information about women's safety and experience on public transport. In a survey conducted by the School of Statistics of the University of Costa Rica in the "Actualidades Survey" (University of Costa Rica, 2015) it is concluded that about 62% of women in the country face some form of violence in public spaces (a figure almost double that of men at 33%), this also includes sexual harassment and other types of violence. The National Commission for Women made progress in creating new norms and protocols that seek to prevent and address this type of violence. INAMU and other relevant government institutions, such as the Ministry of Public Security, the Ministry of Public Health, the Ministry of Education, among others, have developed a protocol for the prevention of sexual harassment applicable to all public spaces, including public transport.

In 2017, in a joint effort, INAMU and Incofer signed an agreement to address and prevent violence against women using the country's train service. The agreement consisted of a campaign to raise awareness about sexual harassment and sexual harassment during train travel and the events of violence experienced in stations. During this time, 150 Incofer officials and other officials from companies that provide services to Incofer were trained.

The implementation of the protocol responds to an environment of widespread violence experienced by women. For example, the German Society for Transportation Research (Vereinigung für Verkehrswissenschaften [GfZ] 2018) conducted a survey to analyze the experiences of women using public transport. Not only do women feel unsafe when using the services (87% of women say they do not feel safe, compared to 42% of men), but they are also more often victims of crime (37% of women were victims of theft compared to 63% of men) and sexual harassment (a result consistent with the information provided by INAMU, about 55% of women compared


---
**Page 130**
---

to 33% of men), In addition, about 35% of women (compared to 23% of men) have been touched without their consent while using public transportation (GIZ, 2018).

Data from the judicial system shows that complaints of violations of the Law on the Condemnation of Violence against Women (PVCM) are registered approximately each year, and among the are the most recorded crimes in the country[1]. While in 2014 19.284 complaints were registered about this type of infraction, by 2019 the complaints had increased to 22.122. The most important crimes are: mistreatment, failure to comply with a protection measure, offense to dignity, and threat against a woman.

On the other hand, the judicial system has seen an increase in cases where sentences have been handed down for PVCM violations since 2014 (judicial Branch). This means that in general there is a tendency to report more frequently this type of crime, and an estimate from the authorities to safeguard security and justice for women. (Figure 10).

Drug use and machismo are causes linked to violence against women. ENVCUM 2004 reports that in 50% of the cases of physical or sexual violence the aggressor had consumed alcohol or some other narcotic substance[1]. In the survey of Perceptions of the Costa Rican Population on Violence Against Women (National Institute of Costa Rica, 2013)[1]the study which was conducted with a sample of 548 women victims of violence against women, 34.6% of the incidents report some type of substance. On the other hand, in the survey of Perceptions of the Costa Rican Population on Violence Against Women (National Institute of Costa Rica, 2013)[1]the study which was conducted with a sample of 548 women victims of violence against women, 34.6% of the incidents report some type of substance.

The effort to improve the collection and systematization of WVC data by improving the administrative records of the institutions that provide services to women victims, conducting specific research, and including questions on this topic in national surveys is a priority for the Ministry of Culture, Youth and Sports. However, it does not have continuity. For example, the Third National Youth Survey[1]of Council of the Young Person - Ministry of Culture, Youth and Sports, 2018)[1]the results of the survey, which were released in 2019, show that 94%


[1] Statistical Yearbook of the Judiciary. 2016
[1] The third delivery started in November 2017

GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN


---
**Page 131**
---

of the total number of people who claim to have been victims of rape (3.7% of the total sample, 2.5% for the third survey), 96% of the total number of people who claim to have been victims of sexual abuse about 1.7%. Of the surveyed population, 80% of the total number of people who report having suffered sexual violence (4.4% of the sample) are women. Also, women report the highest prevalence of psychological and physical violence by their partners: 65.8% of those who report that their partner has ever yelled at them, insulted them, and humiliated them are women (8.2% of the sample), in addition to 62.1% of those who report that their partner has ever hit or pushed them (4.3% of the sample) (Council of the Young Person - Ministry of Culture, Youth and Sports, 2018).

Regarding sexual violence, women report very high percentages of experiencing different expressions of violence, both in the workplace or study, and in other public spaces. The ENSV 2024 concludes that 36.7% of women have been victims of sexual harassment at work or during their studies after the age of 16, that 27.9% have been touched sexually against their will, and 26% have had to force them to have sexual relations or have been raped. Rural women report more exposure to sexual violence (50.4%) than urban women (34.4%).

According to the data on the entry of sexual crimes in the adult criminal prosecutor's offices, the five most prevalent sexual crimes are: sexual abuse against minors and incapable persons, rape, sexual relations with minors, sexual abuse against minors and incapable persons (attempt to), and sexual abuse against adults.

The Judicial Branch and INAMU have made an additional effort to keep a detailed count of the femicides that have occurred in the country under both categories. For example, in the year 2019, 9 femicides were committed according to the LPVCM and 7 additional ones according to the Convention of Belém do Pará *#addition, there has been a reduction in this type of violence since 2015 (Figure 11) (Judicial Branch, 2020).

* The deaths of women at the hands of their spouses or current partners are classified as femicide by Article 21 of the Law on the Elimination of Violence against Women. This article also includes other types of violence that constitute death of woman due to their gender, where there was no marriage or union relationship. For example, these are deaths during custody of a minor (abortion) or after the termination of a de facto union, AIDS, those that occur in the public sphere, as a result of sexual assault.

## During 2020 (up to August), there have been 9 deaths of women classified within what has been called extended femicide. In general, femicides in the country are perpetrated by acquaintances of the victims, and in a minority of cases the relationship of the victim is not known. By 2018, the Judicial Branch (Judicial Power, 2020) reported on the profile of the 26 victims of femicide: the average age of the victims is close to 35 years old; 74% were married or in a relationship with their aggressor; and almost half of the aggressor was living with the victim, in almost 30% of the cases there was already a history of aggression. In addition, 40% of the homicides against women correspond to femicides. In the vast majority of cases, the femicide weapon used is a firearm.

## Within the group of women most affected by violence, girls and adolescents under 19 years of age should be considered a high-risk population, since the legislation does not protect them, or does so only partially. This is particularly worrying when we analyze the figures related to the establishment of inappropriate relationships between minors and adults who are in positions of authority or power, which can lead to sexual violence against them. Many girls and adolescents who suffer acts of violence are detected in their medical care during their regular visits. Data from the Health Surveillance Directorate of the Ministry of Health for the year 2018 show that the group of adolescents between 15 and 19 years of age is the one with the highest number of reported cases.

## Unfortunately, there is no detailed quantitative or qualitative information on the incidence of violence against women or gender-based violence in its characteristics among women belonging to groups that have traditionally been discriminated against, such as indigenous women, women of African descent, women with disabilities, or women of diverse sexual orientation and gender identity and/or expression, all of whom are groups affected by this intersection of various types of inequalities.

# Barriers for Women in the Transport Sector

In Costa Rica, the public transportation system has improved access to goods and services, and above all, it has imposed excess on jobs, which has a great impact on women. Data on urban mobility in Costa Rica show that nearly 60% of public transport users are women. When analyzing differences by sex in the use of public transportation, gender roles must be taken into account. For example, women are generally more likely to make more trips on public transport than men because they are responsible for taking children and elderly people to school or work, as well as other activities associated with caregiving (IDB, 2016). Worldwide, women are more likely to make shorter but more frequent trips than men. Women's travel patterns are more complex and heterogeneous than those of men. The strong persistence of the care-giving role—low- and middle-income countries leads many women to make short trips over a greater distance. Women are also willing to combine transit, make more domestic trips, and travel with children and more packages than men.

Information about women's patterns, preferences and needs is very limited in Costa Rica. At both the user and service provider levels, there is very little sex-disaggregated information or information showing gender-specific needs. The State of the Nation Report (State of the Nation Report (2018) (Estado de la Nación 2018)) shows that women are not aware how they behave regarding the use of alternative means of transport; secondary sources of information should be used, not the transport governing bodies. Specifically, according to the State of the Nation Report 2018 (State of the Nation Report (2018) (Estado de la Nación 2018)), only 3% of women in Costa Rica own a car and only 5% have access to public transportation. Women are more likely than men to use bicycles, but this is not reflected in the data available for the country. Only in the capital province there are more than 17 kilometers dedicated to bicycle paths, while approximately 125,500 daily trips are made, which represent 2% of the total number of trips, and only 20% of these trips are made by women.

Women who use public transport behave similarly in women in other cities around the world. Comparing data available with international information, based on the IOB reading (IDB, 2016)in Latin America, as in almost every country in the world, public transportation systems have not been designed with women's needs and safety in mind. Gaps also exist in both development and deployment of infrastructure for women. For example, in Mexico City, only 35% of women use public transport compared to 60% of men; in Santiago de Chile, only 28% of women use public transport compared to 47% of men; in Bogotá, only 19% of women use public transport compared to 35% of men. In England, where only 57% of women capable of driving have a license, compared to 80% of men. While 18% of men choose to use the bicycle as their most frequent means of transportation, only 12% of women do so. In Mexico City, only 34% of women use public transport in comparison with 57% of men; in Santiago de Chile and Bogotá show that women have less access to cars and motorcycles than men (Santiago: 3.7% women vs. 13% men; Montevideo: 25% women vs. 38% men; and Bogotá: 10% vs. 17%). However, women use public transportation more than men (for women and men, Santiago 68% vs. 45%, in Buenos Aires 52% vs. 48%; in Montevideo 53% vs. 49%; in Bogotá 30% vs. 17%). Women use public transportation more than men (for women and men, Santiago 68% vs. 45%, in Buenos Aires 52% vs. 48%; in Montevideo 53% vs. 49%; in Bogotá 30% vs. 17%). Women use the bicycle much less to get around (3% vs. 10% in Santiago and 1% vs. 5% in Bogotá for women and men).

When these data are contrasted with the reality of Costa Rican women (GIZ, 2018)the results are not much different:
