To show efficiency, the public transportation system must guarantee safe spaces and participation of women. As discussed in section IV, women tend to feel more insecure about using the service than men, inhibiting their free use and therefore imposing a barrier to access different types of markets. The violent practices that women suffer on a daily basis can be perpetuated in new forms of mobility, both unimodal and multimodal, and there is a need to provide tools, infrastructure and protocols for the prevention of violence against women.

The survey conducted by the Comptroller General of the Republic (CGR) (EGB, 2019) reports that women perceive transportation as one of the top priorities in their lives. Figure 12 shows the perceptions of personal priorities in public services, in addition to the main basic services (not included in figure 12). Women placed comfort, safety, health and public transportation as their top options. Although men represent more than half of the population using public transport services, they are not the users with the highest needs for this sector; it is also highly dominated by women, reflecting the need to design and implement actions to attract more women to the challenging and changing sector as users and service providers.

Figure 13: Perceptions of personal priorities for public services (Costa Rica 2018)

| % of the sample (%) | Men | Women |
|---|---|---|
| Security | 7.5% | 19.4% |
| Health | 16.5% | 31.7% |
| Public transportation | 20.5% | 18.1% |
| Employment | 15.0% | 9.8% |

Source: Own elaboration with data from CGR, 2019

Women, although in greater need given the lack of driver's licenses, use the train less than men. According to data from the CGR (2019), women are not the ones who use the train most often in the country; 57% of users are men and they are more frequent users. Women use the bus as a substitute for other means such as taxis, cabs, or other similar services. Graph 14 shows the distribution of means of transport according to gender. Women use the means of bus, car, Uber and bicycle much more than men. With regard to the bicycle, the information provided is different from that which the State of the Nation had captured, and this discrepancy can be attributed to the different methodological approaches used.

Women depend on the public transport sector for their mobility and, when analyzing the ownership of private vehicles, important differences are found at the territorial level in the cantons served by the train. Ownership of cars and motorcycles varies among cantons. For example, in the direct cantons of Belén, San José (with a high density of population and with good road infrastructure), and Escazú (with railway infrastructure is located), while in San José and Alajuela it is over ten percentage points lower (both cantons have direct access to the train but are at the head of the province). These differences are, if possible, more pronounced between cantons: while just over 30% of households in Alajuela have a car, this percentage is twice as high in Escazú. Moreover, ownership per household also varies between cantons, although to a lesser extent, and in addition, car and motorcycle ownership per canton does not seem to be correlated (0.09 [between -1 and 1]) (Figure 15).

Poverty is a determining factor in access to personal transportation vehicles. A key factor in explaining this variation among cantons is the gap or severity of poverty in the households of each canton. As can be seen in Figure 16 and Figure 17, the cantons with the largest poverty gap tend to have fewer cars, and it is argued that multidimensional poverty measurements can also encompass both aspects of mobility and added factors of poverty. In particular, the case of Alajuela is an example of the above, since it has the worst poverty gap among all cantons in Costa Rica.

The results show that the cantons with the highest poverty gaps are those with fewer cars per household according to the graph are Desamparados, San José and Goicoechea. At first glance, the results do not seem to indicate that there are significant differences between direct and indirect cantons in terms of poverty and car ownership.

## Head of household and mobility factors at the cantonal level

In addition to these descriptive statistics and their impact on car ownership, it is useful to use a regression model which takes into account head of household and household size. This can be used for understanding the mobility of household members through car or motorcycle ownership.

The estimated models of household headship and mobility are as follows:

1. **homicar** = β0 + β1jeffem + β2occupy (1)
2. **homicar** = β0 + β1jeffem + β2occupy (2)
3. **homicar** = β0 + β1jeffem + β2occupy (3)
4. **hogarcarmoto** = β0 + β1jeffem + β2occupy (4)
5. **hogarcarmoto** = β0 + β1jeffem + β2occupy (5)
6. **hogarcarmoto** = β0 + β1jeffem + β2occupy (6)

Results: While female or male leadership does not clearly explain motorcycle ownership by household, it does explain car ownership in the household, which in turn can influence household mobility.

## Gender Analysis and Action Plan

With the following variables:
- **Jeffem** = percentage of households led by a woman
- **Jeffemo** = percentage of households headed by a woman without a spouse
- **Jeffemuno** = percentage of households led by a woman or a man
- **Hogarcarmoto** = % of households with a car
- **Hogarcarmoto** = % of households with a motorcycle
- **Ocupyo** = average number of occupants per household


---
**Page 139**
---

Taking an average canton, for each percentage point more households with a woman in the lead, it can be estimated that the percentage of cars owned by women will increase by ~1.34 percentage points, and by ~1.71% in men's households. In a household without a spouse, in particular taking into account these data[1], it is expected that a canton with 27% of women heading households will have an average of nine percentage points more cars than one with 20% of women heading households. The difference between households headed by women and men also serves to explain car ownership in the home: for every percentage point increase in the proportion of women-headed households, the number of cars owned by women increases by 1.34 percentage points; conversely, when the number of cars will increase by 0.73 percentage points in a given canton.

A correlation matrix can help to explore preliminarily which pairs of variables are most related to each other. That is, which individual variables have the most influence on other individual variables.

After making the general correlation matrix, all those values that indicate a correlation higher than 0.8 over 1 or -1 are highlighted in green and then in red.

In terms of gender, the difference between male and female employment has a high correlation with the poverty gap, average schooling and also the difference in the percentage of male and female heads of household. The latter variable also correlates, but somewhat less strictly, with the poverty gap and average schooling.


| occupation/female | Poverty Reduction Strategy Paper | average schooling | homecare |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0.8793 | 1 | -0.9136 | -0.9316 |
| 0.5285 | 1 | -0.8529 | -0.8481 |
| 0.8002 | 1 | 0.1663 | 0.1518 |
| 0.6151 | 0.1363 | -0.6854 | 0.4427 |

In other words, what underlies these preliminary calculations is:

- That the greater the difference between male and female employment, the greater the poverty gap and vice versa.
- That the higher the average schooling, the smaller the difference between male and female employment, and the poverty gap, and vice versa.

[1] The same ambient is used in statistical terms, although the results obtained are valid enough to take into account the relationships obtained. These conclusions should be supported in subsequent studies with larger and more granular series.

* That households with deprivation are highly correlated positively with high levels of poverty and negatively with schooling Therefore, the greater the level of schooling, the less poverty is associated.
* That, individually, there is no variable that correlates strongly with the Gini index, although households with deficiencies do have a correlation greater than 0.5.

This exercise does not explain or pretend to explain the origin or direction of causality, but merely emphasizes that there are significant correlations between some variables.

The relationships between these variables give an indication of which variables to prioritize when performing regressions. It is important to note that the results may vary depending on whether one performs regressions with sets of variables in a certain order or when computing regressions of various types with sets of variables. In addition, some variables may not be individually related but are collectively related; for which more complex explorations are necessary.

Two variables that are interesting to visualize graphically are the difference between male and female occupations and poverty gaps. The poverty gap is represented by the following graph, the shape of the poverty growth line (orange dotted line), coincides with that of the difference between male and female occupations in a canton. In other words, where the difference between employed men and women is greater, there are more poor people. This is the case for cantons such as Montes de Oca, Alajuela, and San José. A seno contrario, Montes de Oca, Escazú and Curridabat are the three cantons with the least difference between male and female employment rates, and also have some of the lowest poverty gaps in the country.

Finally, selecting the three cantons with the largest and smallest poverty gaps, and studying other individually related variables, can help to see what differences exist between these cantons, regardless of the socioeconomic gap. As can be seen in the graph below, which includes five variables, the cantons of Pola, Paralio and Oreamuno, where there are high-poverty gaps, also tend to have households with more deficiencies in terms of schooling and income. In contrast, the three cantons with the largest poverty gap inserted in the labor market than the three cantons with the lowest poverty gap in the sample (Mieres de Oca, Belén and San Pablo). In addition, the poorest cantons also have fewer cars, with a difference of almost 20 percentage points with respect to the richest ones.

Without access to private vehicles, women use public transport services more, yet are often excluded for reasons associated with their safety. Sometimes this is a result of high rates of sexual harassment in mass transit (Kathleen M. Bostic, 2016; Milosavljevic, Jovicic, Milosevic, Milosavljevic and Djoric 2016). Affordability of transport is a challenge, as travel expenses consume 30 percent or more of the income of the poor in the region, adding to the already high costs of travel time (Kathleen M. Bostic, 2016; Milosavljevic, Jovicic, Milosevic, Milosavljevic and Djoric 2016). Women report that nearly 70 percent of women report being assaulted in public transportation systems and on average earn less than men.

The government and other non-governmental actors are implementing actions that contribute to the inclusion of the gender perspective through specific actions that seek to create spaces for women in the sector. Based on a review of OIC (2018), the following are some of the most relevant initiatives that contribute to closing gender gaps or contribute to the empowerment of women within the sector (Table 3).

The country still needs more data on women's participation in the sector, including information on how many women are in decision-making positions, what percentage represent as service providers or users of public transportation such as buses and trains, and what are the perceptions and cases of violence.

Based on the analysis of available information, the transport sector in Costa Rica is still dominated by men in all spheres of decision-making and the provision of transport services, including the train network. Initiatives that consider gender perspectives are dispersed and not institutionalized within the context of Costa Rican transport, and projects such as the one proposed could generate positive impacts on women.

Based on the analysis of available information, the transport sector in Costa Rica is still dominated by men in all spheres of decision-making and the provision of transport services, including the train network. Initiatives that consider gender perspectives are dispersed and not institutionalized within the context of Costa Rican transport, and projects such as the one proposed could generate positive impacts on women.

Currently, INCOFER has 10 positions (including the executive president), out of which 42% (4 of 7 positions) were awarded to women. However, there is no public information on the constitution of human resources within INCOFER, which limits the analysis of women's participation within the institute itself.

# Gender Action Plan

The following are the considerations that will be taken into account for the development of the gender action plan. This action plan seeks to incorporate activities that will improve women's participation as users and beneficiaries in all public services provided by the institution. These activities must be led in an inter-institutional manner and at different levels of public administration.

Given the proximity of changes of the political cycle to ensure sustainability, and continuity of the project, it is recommended that the new authorities are informed about the progress and impact of this initiative should be presented and discussed. Periodic meetings on webinars, workshops, and monitoring update meetings are also suggested to coordinate, adapt, scale and sustain activities.

In case, an institution is dissolved, the activities should be delegated to another relevant organization that have similar capacities.

Activities related to violence against women are not presented/ pretend to be implemented in isolation. The umbrella project incorporates activities and plan to support the construction of infrastructure that aim to prevent and address violence against women in transport.

After the identification of relevant institutions and initiatives, actions and indicators will be proposed for the following dimensions:

Dimension 1 - Mobility and Safety:
- Consideration of gender-differentiated mobility patterns, including intermodality of different media
- Prevention of violence against women in public spaces and in train service

Dimension 2 - Accessibility:
- Gaps in access to transport systems and means of transport represent a major barrier to women's access to the economic, political and social spheres
- The new or rehabilitated infrastructure should be designed to take into account the needs of women and to safeguard their physical autonomy.


---
**Page 146**
---

Dimension 3 - Participation

- The possibility of generating direct and indirect jobs for women in the public transport sector, in particular on the train.
- Creating spaces for women to participate at all levels of decision-making.

The CABEI gender specialist will play an important role during the project implementation of the project. The Special Committee is being committed towards gender equality (and aligned across institutions) are being met within the role of CABEI of supervising INCODER as implementer of Component 3.

In order to avoid costs and economic losses, it is necessary to identify and address the problem in the most expeditious manner. It is proposed to design activities that promote the training of the personnel that will allow them to identify the violence events and provide tools to facilitate the approach of the women to the specific services in case of an attack. On the other hand, it is also necessary to ensure the implementation of communication tools and campaigns directed to men and women in order to prevent and attend to violence against women. There is not a lot of evidence on the effectiveness of mobile applications against women (WAW) to change social norms; however, the app mentioned will aim to capture information in its use. As an action act when there are users in danger. The app will provide guidelines to file a complaint and seek help for with relevant institutions. It could be used by any user who has been victimized or is currently being victimized. For example, it can be used by victims of violence in public transport. It could be either a first app developed for Android and iPhone or an app that is co-developed with the Ministry of Security; the app will be available to all users. The police officers/special task force will be located around the area of the event. The officers will arrive where the victim is at the moment and they can file the complaint and get assistance from the police. It is planned to implement a pilot project at first and then expand, and adapt based on an evaluation

It is necessary to ensure that women can access different types of services, not only when using the train or waiting inside the stations, but also the connection between the station and other public spaces, where they are protected by their physical autonomy. # GENDER ANALYSIS AND ACTION PLAN

| Model Title/Description | Type of Implementation | Description of Target Group and Context | Expected Outcomes | Monitoring Indicators | Notes |
|-------------------------|------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------|-----------------------|------|
| **Model 1: Community-based** | **Community-based** | **Target Group:** Women living in rural areas with limited access to education, healthcare, and economic opportunities. Context: Lack of awareness about gender equality issues and limited resources for women's empowerment. | Improved knowledge on gender equality among women; increased participation of women in community decision-making processes; enhanced access to education, healthcare, and economic opportunities for women. | Number of women trained in gender equality; number of women participating in community meetings; increase in women's enrollment in schools; improvement in health indicators for women; increase in women's income generation activities. | **Note:** Ensure that the training programs are inclusive and address specific needs of different age groups, disabilities, and ethnicities within the target group. |
| **Model 2: School-based** | **School-based** | **Target Group:** Girls aged between 10-18 years in secondary schools with high dropout rates due to gender discrimination. Context: Lack of awareness about gender equality issues among students and teachers; limited access to education for girls. | Increased knowledge on gender equality among students and teachers; reduced dropout rates among girls; improved participation of girls in extracurricular activities and leadership roles. | Number of students trained in gender equality; number of teachers trained in gender equality; reduction in dropout rates among girls; increase in female enrollment in extracurricular activities and leadership roles. | **Note:** Collaborate with parents, community leaders, and local government to ensure the sustainability of the program. |
| **Model 3: Workplace-based** | **Workplace-based** | **Target Group:** Women working in informal sectors such as agriculture, domestic work, and small-scale businesses. Context: Lack of awareness about gender equality issues among employers; limited access to economic opportunities for women. | Improved knowledge on gender equality among employers and employees; increased participation of women in decision-making processes at the workplace; enhanced access to economic opportunities for women. | Number of employers trained in gender equality; number of women participating in decision-making processes at the workplace; increase in women's income generation activities; improvement in working conditions for women. | **Note:** Provide training programs that address specific needs and challenges faced by women in different sectors, such as agricultural cooperatives or domestic work. |
| **Model 4: Media-based** | **Media-based** | **Target Group:** General public through various media platforms (TV, radio, newspapers). Context: Lack of awareness about gender equality issues; limited representation of women in media content. | Increased knowledge on gender equality among the general public; improved representation of women in media content; reduced stereotypes and biases against women. | Number of media outlets that include gender equality content; number of stories featuring women as protagonists or experts; reduction in negative portrayal of women in media. | **Note:** Collaborate with media organizations to ensure the sustainability of the program and promote positive representations of women in media content. |

# Annex B: Stakeholder Meetings

In the development of the Funding Proposal to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund, several meetings were held with stakeholders regarding the gender issue. In this sense, the following actions were carried out:

## 1. Meeting June 14, 2020

The objective of this meeting was to identify synergies, coordinate activities, list stakeholders and review sources of information with the ongoing GEF/UNDP study (see file 1). In this regard, it was identified that the Gender Plan developed for the GEF/UNDP study was discussed with different stakeholders.

## 2. Meeting August 18, 2020

The objective of this meeting was to identify the actions carried out by INCOFER and the National Women's Institute (INAMU) in gender in the operation of the Train (see file 3):

- **INCOFER** as part of the Institutional Commission for Gender Equality
- **INCOFER** and the National Women's Institute signed an agreement to contribute to the Eradication of Violence against Women and the Promotion of Equal Relationships.
- **INCOFER** and Transfraco & Cía. S.A. personnel were trained on the Prevention of Sexual Harassment in Public Spaces, "Addressing sexual harassment in transit cars internally and externally" Campaign for the Prevention of Sexual Harassment in Public Spaces", internal advertising is installed in stations.

## 3. Meeting September 19, 2020

Since August 2018 the Multilevel Technical Table (Mesa de Técnica Multinivel, MTM) has been created as a coordination and participation mechanism to define, enable and implement the urban and territorial planning conditions for the implementation of the GEF/UNDP project. The MTM is composed by the main actors with influence of the TRP project and its stations, as well as the accessibility infrastructure generated by the municipalities, such as sidewalks, boulevards, bike paths and parking lots. The MTM is coordinated by the Ministry of Housing and Human Settlements (MINHUH), it is in the governing body for land management in Costa Rica.

Other members of the MTM are: the Institute of National Urban Development (INUVI), the Institute of Municipal Development and Advisory Services (IAMF) and the National Union of Local Governments (UNGLL), the Ministry of Planning, the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE), the Ministry of Public Works and Transport (MPPT), the Ministry of Tourism (MINTUR), the Ministry of Public Works and Transport (MOPFT). On the private sector side, the Chamber of Commerce, the Chamber of Construction and the Council for Real Estate Development (CCDI) participate, as well as international cooperation actors such as the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), the World Bank (WB), the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF), and representatives of the academic sector and civil society (urban collectives, cooperatives associations, impacted population).

On September 15, 2020, Gutierrez Consulting presented to the MTM the progress of the project funding proposal, including the socialization process for the GEF/UNDP project and the proposed Gender Action Plan. More than 50 people attended the meeting from entities listed above. Due to Covid-19 restrictions, this meeting was conducted through zoom (virtual platform).
